The **Miami Hurricanes football** team represents the [University of Miami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Miami) in [college football](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_football). The Hurricanes compete in the [NCAA's Football Bowl Subdivision](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA_Division_I_Football_Bowl_Subdivision) (FBS). The team is a member of the [Atlantic Coast Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_Coast_Conference) (ACC), one of the four [power conferences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_conferences) in college football. The program began in 1926 and joined the ACC in 2004, competing in the conference's Coastal Division from 2005 until the ACC eliminated divisions in 2023.[^cite_note-3]

The Miami Hurricanes are among the most storied and decorated football programs in NCAA history. Miami has won five [AP national championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AP_Trophy) in [1983](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team), [1987](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1987_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team), [1989](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1989_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team), [1991](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team), and [2001](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2001_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team).[^cite_note-4] Miami is ranked fifth on the list of all-time Associated Press National Poll Championships, tied with [USC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USC_Trojans_football) and behind [Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alabama_Crimson_Tide_Football), [Notre Dame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Notre_Dame_Fighting_Irish_football), [Oklahoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oklahoma_Sooners_football), and [Ohio State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ohio_State_Buckeyes_football).[^cite_note-5] Two Hurricanes, [Vinny Testaverde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vinny_Testaverde) in [1986](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1986_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team) and [Gino Torretta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gino_Torretta) in [1992](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team), have won the [Heisman Trophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heisman_Trophy). As of 2023, eight University of Miami players and four coaches have been inducted into the [College Football Hall of Fame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_Football_Hall_of_Fame). Among players, [Bennie Blades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bennie_Blades), [Don Bosseler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don_Bosseler), [Ted Hendricks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ted_Hendricks), [Russell Maryland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russell_Maryland), [Ed Reed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ed_Reed), Vinny Testaverde, Gino Torretta, and [Arnold Tucker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arnold_Tucker) have been inducted. Coaches inducted include [Dennis Erickson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dennis_Erickson), [Andy Gustafson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andy_Gustafson), [Jack Harding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jack_Harding), and [Jimmy Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Johnson_\(American_football_coach\)).[^cite_note-6]

As of the end of the [2025 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team), the Miami Hurricanes have compiled a record of 688–394–19 since the program's 1926 founding. In addition to its five national championships, the University of Miami has won nine conference championships and appeared in 46 major [bowl games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bowl_games).[^cite_note-7]

As of 2024, eleven Miami Hurricanes have been inducted into the [NFL](21211.md)'s [Pro Football Hall of Fame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro_Football_Hall_of_Fame): [Jim Otto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Otto) in 1980, Ted Hendricks in 1990, [Jim Kelly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Kelly) in 2002, [Michael Irvin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Irvin) in 2007, [Cortez Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cortez_Kennedy) in 2012, [Warren Sapp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warren_Sapp) in 2013, [Ray Lewis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ray_Lewis) in 2018, Ed Reed in 2019, [Edgerrin James](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edgerrin_James) in 2020, and [Devin Hester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devin_Hester) and [Andre Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andre_Johnson) in 2024.

Since [2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team), the University of Miami has played its home games at [Hard Rock Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hard_Rock_Stadium) in [Miami Gardens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami_Gardens,_Florida), roughly 22 miles (35 km) north of the university's primary campus in [Coral Gables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coral_Gables,_Florida). Prior to 2008, from 1937 until 2007, Miami played their home games at the [Miami Orange Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami_Orange_Bowl) in the [Little Havana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Havana) section of [Miami](53846.md), which was demolished in 2008 after 71 years of use by the NFL's [Miami Dolphins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami_Dolphins), the Hurricanes, and for other athletic and entertainment purposes.

In December 2021, the University of Miami announced the appointment of [Mario Cristobal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mario_Cristobal) as the team's new coach. Cristobal signed a 10-year, \$80 million contract with the Hurricanes.[^cite_note-8]

## History

See also: [List of Miami Hurricanes football seasons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Miami_Hurricanes_football_seasons)

### Early history (1926–1978)

Main article: [Miami Hurricanes football (1926 to 1978)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami_Hurricanes_football_\(1926_to_1978\))



The first University of Miami football team in [1926](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1926_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team)



[Cub Buck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cub_Buck), a former [NFL](21211.md) player with the [Canton Bulldogs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canton_Bulldogs) and [Green Bay Packers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Bay_Packers), coached the University of Miami football team in the [1927](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1927_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team) and [1928](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1928_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team) seasons.

#### 1920s

The [University of Miami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Miami) football program began with a freshman team in [1926](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1926_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team).[^cite_note-9] The program's first game was a 7–0 victory over [Rollins College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rollins_Tars) on October 23, [1926](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1926_college_football_season) before 304 fans.[^cite_note-2007_media-10] Under the guidance of head coach [Howard "Cub" Buck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cub_Buck), a former [NFL](21211.md) player, the freshman team posted an undefeated 8–0 record in its inaugural season.[^cite_note-11] Two of Miami's wins in 1926 came against the [University of Havana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Havana),[^cite_note-mg158-12] one on [Thanksgiving Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thanksgiving_\(United_States\)) in [Miami](53846.md) and one in [Havana](49719.md), Cuba, on [Christmas Day](6237.md). The Hurricanes won both games against the University of Havana by an identical shutout score of 23–0. The Hurricanes won their last home game of its inaugural 1926 season against Howard College, now [Samford University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samford_Bulldogs_football), 9–7, at the University of Miami's University Stadium. Its win over Howard College was also the first Hurricane football game played on [New Year's Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Year's_Day).[^cite_note-13]

The following year, in 1927, the team adopted the "[Miami Hurricanes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami_Hurricanes)" as the name for its athletic teams. The origins of the name are not exactly clear; some reports suggest the name was a reference to the devastating power of the [1926 hurricane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1926_Miami_hurricane) that postponed the program's first game by a month, and others that it was suggested by a player in response to rumors that university officials wanted to name the team after local [flora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flora) or [fauna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fauna).[^cite_note-team_name-14][^cite_note-15]

Varsity competition began in [1927](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1927_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team), with the Hurricanes beating Rollins, 39–3, in its first game and going on to a 3–6–1 record.[^cite_note-16] The team improved to 4–4–1 in [1928](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1928_college_football_season),[^cite_note-17] but the program fired Buck, who was replaced prior to the [1929 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1929_college_football_season) with [J. Burton Rix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._Burton_Rix), previously head coach at [Southern Methodist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Methodist_Mustangs_football).[^cite_note-mg158-12] Rix's arrival was funded by a group of local businessmen.[^cite_note-18]

#### 1930s

Rix was replaced the following season, in [1930](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1930_college_football_season), by [Ernest Brett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernest_E._Brett). The Hurricanes played [Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_Owls_football) in its first game outside [the South](179553.md), losing 34–0 in a game played in [Atlantic City, New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_City,_New_Jersey).[^cite_note-mg158-12] On October 31, 1930, the Hurricanes played in one of the nation's first night games, facing [Bowden College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bowden_College) in Miami.[^cite_note-19]

Brett only lasted one year, and [Tom McCann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_McCann) became the program's fourth head coach in [1931](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1931_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team).[^cite_note-20] Under McCann, the football program experienced its most successful seasons to that date.

Following a difficult first year, the Hurricanes recorded a winning record in the [1932 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1932_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team) and served as host to the inaugural Palm Festival, later renamed the [Orange Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orange_Bowl), where it defeated [Manhattan College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manhattan_College) 7–0 at Moore Park in Miami.[^cite_note-mg158-12] A 5–1–2 campaign and another Palm Festival berth followed in [1933](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1933_college_football_season), and in [1934](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1934_college_football_season), the program played in its first official [bowl game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bowl_game), losing to [Bucknell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bucknell_Bison_football) in the first Orange Bowl, 26–0.[^cite_note-mg158-12] In 1935, a group of Hurricanes' football supporters sought to hire [Red Grange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Grange) as coach,[^cite_note-21] but the move was vetoed by President [Bowman Foster Ashe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bowman_Foster_Ashe) in part because of what was perceived as the excessive \$7,500 salary that Grange sought.[^cite_note-22] [Irl Tubbs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irl_Tubbs) took over as head coach in 1935. The Hurricanes compiled an 11–5–2 record in his two seasons,[^cite_note-23] but the team failed to reach a [bowl game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bowl_game) in either year.[^cite_note-mg165-24]

After [Irl Tubbs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irl_Tubbs) resigned following the [1936 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1936_college_football_season) to become head coach at [Iowa](963244.md),[^cite_note-25] [Jack Harding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jack_Harding) was hired to serve as both head football coach and [athletic director](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athletic_director) at the University of Miami.[^cite_note-2007_media-10] In 1937, the Hurricanes moved into the brand new Burdine Municipal Stadium, renamed the [Orange Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami_Orange_Bowl) in 1959, located in [Little Havana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Havana) just west of [Downtown Miami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Downtown_Miami).[^cite_note-2007_media-10] The following year, Miami played archrival [Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1938_Florida_Gators_football_team) for the first time, defeating the Gators 19–7 at [Florida Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Hill_Griffin_Stadium), and won the program's first [Southern Intercollegiate Athletic Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Intercollegiate_Athletic_Association) title with an 8–2 record.[^cite_note-26]

#### 1940s



The Miami Hurricanes hosting [Texas Tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Tech_Red_Raiders_football) at Burdine Stadium, later renamed the [Miami Orange Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami_Orange_Bowl), in [Miami](53846.md), on October 31, 1941

Harding led the Hurricanes to an eight-win season in 1941 and a seven-win campaign in 1942 prior to being called away for service in [World War II](32927.md).[^cite_note-2007_media-10] [Eddie Dunn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eddie_Dunn_\(American_football\)), a former star running back for the Hurricanes under Harding, stepped into the void and served as head coach during Harding's two-year absence during World War II.[^cite_note-27] In 1943, the Hurricanes won five games,[^cite_note-28] but they faltered the following year, in 1944, winning just once and losing seven and tying one game.[^cite_note-29]

Harding returned in 1945, and the Hurricanes improved to 9–1–1, and returned to the [Orange Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orange_Bowl_\(game\)) for the first time since 1934, where they defeated [Holy Cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Cross_Crusaders_football) 13–6.[^cite_note-1946_orange_bowl-30]

#### 1950s

Harding was succeeded by [Andy Gustafson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andy_Gustafson), who introduced a "drive series" offense, which featured an [option-oriented attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Option_offense) from the [Split-T](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Split-T) formation that relied on [zone blocking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zone_blocking) and either a [fullback](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fullback_\(American_football\)) fake or carry on every play.[^cite_note-gustafson-31][^cite_note-32] Under Gustafson, the Hurricanes went 9–1–1 in [1951](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1951_college_football_season), including a 35–13 win in its first-ever game against rival [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_State_Seminoles_football). The same season, the Hurricanes produced their first [All-American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_Football_All-America_Team), [Al Carapella](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Carapella), and returned to the [Orange Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orange_Bowl_\(game\)), losing to [Clemson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clemson_Tigers_football) 15–14.[^cite_note-2007_media-10][^cite_note-bowl_history-33] The [following season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1952_college_football_season), the Hurricanes won eight games and went to a bowl game in consecutive years for the first time in school history, shutting out Clemson 14–0 in a rematch at the [Gator Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gator_Bowl).

#### 1960s

In the later years of Gustafson's tenure, two-time All-America quarterback [George Mira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Mira) guided the Hurricanes to berths in the [1961](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1961_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team) [Liberty Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberty_Bowl) and the [1962](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1962_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team) [Gotham Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gotham_Bowl), where they lost both games.[^cite_note-34][^cite_note-35]

In [1963](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1963_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team), the team struggled to a 3–7 record.[^cite_note-36] Nevertheless, Mira, who set many of the school's passing records during his four years at Miami, appeared on the cover of *[Sports Illustrated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sports_Illustrated)* and finished fifth in the [Heisman Trophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heisman_Trophy) voting as a senior.[^cite_note-2007_media-10][^cite_note-37][^cite_note-38]

Following the season, Gustafson decided to step down as head coach and [Charlie Tate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlie_Tate), an assistant at [Georgia Tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_Tech_Yellow_Jackets_football), was hired to replace him.[^cite_note-39] Gustafson has the Hurricane record for most years as head coach (16) and most wins (93).[^cite_note-40][^cite_note-41][^cite_note-42] [Charlie Tate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlie_Tate)'s first seasons at Miami were uneventful, with the team posting a 4–5–1 record in [1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1964_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team)[^cite_note-43] and a reverse 5–4–1 record in [1965](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1965_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team).[^cite_note-44] [1966](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1966_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team) brought the arrival of [defensive end](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defensive_end) [Ted Hendricks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ted_Hendricks), the only three-time [All-American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_Football_All-America_Team) in school history,[^cite_note-45] and [the Hurricanes won eight games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1966_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team), earning a trip to the [Liberty Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1966_Liberty_Bowl), where they defeated No. 9 [Virginia Tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Tech_Hokies_football), 14–7.[^cite_note-2007_media-10]

In December 1966, the program was [integrated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_integration) when [African-American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American) [wide receiver](492476.md) Ray Bellamy signed a letter of intent to play football at the university.[^cite_note-cane_mutiny-46] The Hurricanes returned to bowl play in [1967](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1967_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team), appearing in the [Bluebonnet Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bluebonnet_Bowl), where they lost to [Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Buffaloes_football) 31–21.[^cite_note-47] The Hurricanes had a 5–5–0 season in 1968[^cite_note-48] and 4–6–0 in 1969.[^cite_note-49]

#### 1970s

Tate resigned as head coach two games into the [1970 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1970_NCAA_University_Division_football_season), later citing burn out and fatigue from "fighting the money battle and other battles" as the basis for his decision.[^cite_note-50] [Walt Kichefski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walt_Kichefski), an assistant on Tate's staff, was elevated to head coach in the wake of Tate's resignation and coached the team to a 3–8 record in 1970.[^cite_note-51] He was not retained the following season.

On December 20, 1970, [Fran Curci](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran_Curci), a former [All-American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_Football_All-America_Team) quarterback for the Hurricanes under [Andy Gustafson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andy_Gustafson), was named as the program's new head coach.[^cite_note-52] Curci's [1971 team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1971_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team) improved by a game, but rival [Florida Gators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1971_Florida_Gators_football_team) defeated the Hurricanes in a game that came to be known as "the Gator Flop".[^cite_note-53] The Gators led throughout the game and were up 45–8 when [John Reaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Reaves) threw an interception to the Hurricanes' defense with little time left in the fourth quarter. Reaves needed just 15 more passing yards to break the [NCAA](60706.md) record for career passing yards.[^cite_note-cm4-54]

[Lou Saban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lou_Saban), formerly head coach of the [NFL](21211.md)'s [Buffalo Bills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buffalo_Bills), [Denver Broncos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denver_Broncos), and [Boston Patriots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Patriots),[^cite_note-cane_mutiny-46] was hired on December 27, 1976, as the team's new head coach.[^cite_note-2007_media-10] The Hurricanes won only three games in [1977](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1977_NCAA_Division_I_football_season), but Saban was able to put together a well-regarded recruiting class that included future [Pro Football Hall of Fame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro_Football_Hall_of_Fame) quarterback [Jim Kelly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Kelly) of [East Brady, Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Brady,_Pennsylvania).[^cite_note-cane_mutiny-46] Kelly had been recruited by [Penn State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penn_State_Nittany_Lions_football) as a [linebacker](492475.md) and agreed to come to Miami after Saban promised him he would play quarterback.[^cite_note-cane_mutiny-46] Among the other 30 signees in Saban's first recruiting class were 11 future NFL players.[^cite_note-2007_media-10] The Hurricanes improved by three games in Saban's second season and [Ottis Anderson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottis_Anderson) emerged as an NFL talent. Anderson became the first University of Miami running back to rush for 1,000 yards in a season and led the team in rushing for three straight seasons from 1977 through 1979. Anderson set numerous school rushing records and was the Hurricanes' career rushing leader until 2014, when he was overtaken by [Duke Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duke_Johnson).[^cite_note-2007_media-10] After just two seasons, Saban left after the 1978 season to take the head coaching position at [Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Army_Black_Knights_football).[^cite_note-cane_mutiny-46][^cite_note-55]

### Howard Schnellenberger era (1979–1983)



[Howard Schnellenberger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard_Schnellenberger) (right), who coached the Miami Hurricanes from 1979 to 1983 and led the [University of Miami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Miami) to their first national championship in [1983](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983_NCAA_Division_I-A_football_season)

In the wake of Saban's departure, the extensive coaching upheaval the Hurricanes faced in the prior decade, and various fiscal challenges then confronting the university, the university's board of trustees considered holding a vote on whether to reclassify the football program at the [Division I-AA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Division_I-AA) level, or even eliminate it altogether.[^cite_note-cm18-56] University of Miami executive vice president [John L. Green Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_L._Green_Jr.) successfully convinced the board to give [Division I-A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Division_I-A) football another shot. To replace Saban, the Hurricanes hired [Howard Schnellenberger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard_Schnellenberger), the former head coach of the [Baltimore Colts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltimore_Colts) from 1973 to 1974 and the [offensive coordinator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Offensive_coordinator) for the [Miami Dolphins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami_Dolphins) under [Don Shula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don_Shula). In [1972](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1972_Miami_Dolphins_season), Shula and Schnellenberger led the Dolphins to the first and only undefeated, [Super Bowl](27718.md)-winning season in NFL history.[^cite_note-57][^cite_note-58][^cite_note-cm18-56] In addition to his NFL experience, Schnellenberger had played [end](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/End_\(gridiron_football\)) at [Kentucky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kentucky_Wildcats_football) from 1952 to 1956 under head coach [Bear Bryant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bear_Bryant) and then served as Bryant's offensive coordinator at [Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alabama_Crimson_Tide_football) from 1961 to 1965, helping the Crimson Tide win three national championships in 1961, 1964, and 1965.[^cite_note-59]

At the outset of his tenure, [Howard Schnellenberger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard_Schnellenberger) announced to his staff and players his intention to win a [national championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA_Division_I_FBS_National_Football_Championship) within five years, a bold claim for a program that was seemingly on its last legs.[^cite_note-cm19-60] His five-year plan had two main priorities: installing a [pro-style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro_Style) passing offense and upgrading the talent level on the roster through a new recruiting strategy aimed at heavily targeting the best local talent from the city of Miami and the region of [South Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Florida), a strategy that would drastically change national recruiting in the state of [Florida](18933066.md) in the coming years.[^cite_note-cm22-61] On the recruiting front, Schnellenberger spoke of mining the "State of Miami", which entailed fencing off the fertile [South Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Florida) recruiting base from other programs and cherry-picking the rest of the nation for a few choice recruits.[^cite_note-cm24-62] To help with the new pro-style offense, Schnellenberger hired former [Baltimore Colts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltimore_Colts) quarterback [Earl Morrall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earl_Morrall) as a volunteer quarterbacks coach.[^cite_note-cm22-61] Schnellenberger also sought to exploit the freedom provided by Miami's [independent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA_Division_I_FBS_independent_schools) schedule to gain "intersectional exposure" and make the program "national".[^cite_note-cm22-61]

On the field, Miami went 5–6 in [Schnellenberger's debut season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team),[^cite_note-63] which was highlighted by a 26–10 upset win at No. 16 [Penn State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penn_State_Nittany_Lions) in which [redshirt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redshirt_\(college_sports\)) freshman [Jim Kelly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Kelly) threw for 280 yards and three touchdowns in his first career start as Miami's quarterback.[^cite_note-cm31-64]

Schnellenberger set a bowl berth as the goal of the [1980 campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team) and the team made good on its head coach's expectations, winning nine games and earning a trip to the [1981 Peach Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1981_Peach_Bowl_\(January\)), where the Hurricanes defeated [Virginia Tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Tech_Hokies_football) 20–10.[^cite_note-cane_mutiny-46] The bowl berth was Miami's first since 1967 and the team finished the season ranked 18th in both the [AP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AP_Poll) and [Coaches'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coaches'_Poll) Polls.

Miami continued to improve in [1981](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1981_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team), going 9–2[^cite_note-65] and defeating No. 1 [Penn State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penn_State_Nittany_Lions_football) 17–14 in a late-October game at the [Orange Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami_Orange_Bowl).[^cite_note-66] In the season's final game, the Hurricanes topped rival [Notre Dame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Notre_Dame_Fighting_Irish_football) for the first time since 1960, 37–15, finishing the season eighth in the AP Poll.[^cite_note-67]

The [following season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1982_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team), the team finished with four losses following Kelly's shoulder injury.[^cite_note-68] Entering the [1983 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team)—the fifth of Schnellenberger's tenure—the program had to find a replacement for the recently graduated Kelly. Ultimately, Schnellenberger chose [Bernie Kosar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernie_Kosar) as the team's starting quarterback over fellow redshirt freshman [Vinny Testaverde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vinny_Testaverde).[^cite_note-69]

#### 1983 season and first national championship

The [1983 Miami Hurricanes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team) started the season unranked and lost 28–3 at [Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983_Florida_Gators_football_team) in their first game, though Kosar tied [George Mira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Mira)'s single-game school record of 25 pass completions.[^cite_note-cane_mutiny-46] The Hurricanes rallied by winning their next 10 games, including a 20–0 early-season shutout of [Notre Dame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983_Notre_Dame_Fighting_Irish_football_team),[^cite_note-70] and earned a berth to the [1984 Orange Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_Orange_Bowl) to play the undefeated, top-ranked [Nebraska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983_Nebraska_Cornhuskers_football_team) team that had both [Mike Rozier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Rozier) and [Turner Gill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turner_Gill).

The Orange Bowl-berth was Miami's first since 1951, but the program's first national championship remained a long shot, as the Hurricanes entered the game ranked fifth. Miami got much needed help early on New Year's Day when second-ranked [Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Longhorns_football), the nation's other undefeated team, lost in the [Cotton Bowl Classic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_Cotton_Bowl_Classic) and fourth-ranked [Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Fighting_Illini_football) lost in the [Rose Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_Rose_Bowl).[^cite_note-1984_orange-71] Behind Kosar's passing, Miami jumped out to a 17–0 lead, but Nebraska battled back and cut Miami's lead to 31–24 in the fourth quarter.[^cite_note-1984_orange-71] With 48 seconds remaining, Nebraska scored a touchdown to make it 31–30 and as the number one-ranked team in the nation needed only to kick the extra point to tie the game and put itself in position to win the national championship. Nebraska head coach [Tom Osborne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Osborne) elected to go for the win and attempt a two-point conversion instead.[^cite_note-1984_orange-71] On the ensuing play, Miami [safety](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safety_\(American_football_position\)) Kenny Calhoun tipped away Gill's pass to receiver Jeff Smith in the end zone, saving the game and winning Miami the national championship when it leap-frogged No. 3 [Auburn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983_Auburn_Tigers_football_team) to finish first in the final polls.[^cite_note-1984_orange-71] Although Schnellenberger had made good on his five-year plan to win a national championship, he left after the season to accept a head coaching position in the [USFL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USFL).[^cite_note-mediaguide160-72]

### Jimmy Johnson era (1984–1988)



[Jimmy Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Johnson_\(American_football_coach\)), head coach from 1984 to 1989, who led the University of Miami to its second national championship in [1987](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1987_NCAA_Division_I-A_football_season), at [Bagram Airfield](197197.md) in [Afghanistan](737.md) in November 2009



[Jimmy Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Johnson_\(American_football_coach\)) and the [1987 Miami Hurricanes team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1987_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team) present then [President](24113.md) [Ronald Reagan](25433.md) with a University of Miami jersey at the [White House](33057.md) in January 1988 after winning the [1987 national championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1987_NCAA_Division_I-A_football_season)

Two weeks later, athletic director [Sam Jankovich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_Jankovich) hired [Oklahoma State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oklahoma_State_Cowboys_football) head coach [Jimmy Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Johnson_\(American_football_coach\)) to fill the vacancy.[^cite_note-mediaguide160-72] One of Jimmy Johnson's immediate priorities upon taking over as Miami head coach was to switch to a 4–3 defense.[^cite_note-cm55-73] Johnson wanted to implement the change for his first season, but lacking the time, personnel, and staff, he decided to postpone the switch and kept Schnellenberger's 5–2 defensive package for the 1984 season.[^cite_note-cm55-73]

The team struggled to an 8–5 record in Johnson's first season, losing a number of noteworthy games.[^cite_note-74] In the next-to-last game of the regular season, the No. 6 Hurricanes squandered a 31–0 halftime lead against [Maryland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_Maryland_Terrapins_football_team) and lost 42–40 in what was then the biggest comeback in NCAA football history.[^cite_note-maryland_comeback-75] The following week, Miami lost 47–45 when [Boston College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_Boston_College_Eagles_football_team)'s [Doug Flutie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doug_Flutie) connected with [Gerard Phelan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerard_Phelan) for a 48-yard [Hail Mary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hail_Mary_pass) touchdown on the final play in what has been called the [Hail Flutie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hail_Flutie) game.[^cite_note-flutie-76] The Hurricanes ended the season on a three-game losing streak by dropping the [1985 Fiesta Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1985_Fiesta_Bowl) to [UCLA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_UCLA_Bruins_football_team), 39–37, in a game that featured six lead changes.[^cite_note-1985_fiesta-77]

During the off-season, Johnson made a number of coaching changes, facilitating the switch to the 4–3 defense, and junior [Vinny Testaverde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vinny_Testaverde) succeeded early-graduate [Bernie Kosar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernie_Kosar) at quarterback.[^cite_note-cane_mutiny-46]

The [1985 team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1985_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team) opened the season with a loss at [Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1985_Florida_Gators_football_team)[^cite_note-78] before winning their next four games, including a 38–0 win over [Cincinnati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cincinnati_Bearcats_football) that began a then NCAA-record 58 game home winning streak,[^cite_note-79] heading into a matchup at No. 3 [Oklahoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1985_Oklahoma_Sooners_football_team). Facing the nation's top-rated defense, Testaverde amassed 270 yards passing and threw touchdowns to [Michael Irvin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Irvin) and [Brian Blades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brian_Blades), while also running for an additional score, in a 27–14 win over the Sooners.[^cite_note-cane_mutiny-46][^cite_note-miami_oklahoma-80] The Hurricanes ascended to number two in the rankings following a 58–7 victory over [Notre Dame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1985_Notre_Dame_Fighting_Irish_football_team) in the final game of the regular season,[^cite_note-81] earning a trip to the [Sugar Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1986_Sugar_Bowl) to play the No. 8 [Tennessee Volunteers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1985_Tennessee_Volunteers_football_team). With No. 1 [Penn State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1985_Penn_State_Nittany_Lions_football_team) losing to Oklahoma in the [Orange Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orange_Bowl), Miami was in position to capture its second [national championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA_Division_I_FBS_National_Football_Championship), but those hopes were dashed with a lopsided 35–7 loss to Tennessee.[^cite_note-82]

Miami opened [its 1986 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1986_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team) as the third-ranked team in the country and climbed to number two after winning its first three games, setting up a No. 1 vs. No. 2 showdown at the Orange Bowl against top-ranked and defending national champion [Oklahoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1986_Oklahoma_Sooners_football_team).[^cite_note-cane_mutiny-46][^cite_note-miami_oklahoma-80] After much pre-game [trash-talk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trash-talk) between Oklahoma's [Brian Bosworth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brian_Bosworth) and Miami's [Melvin Bratton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melvin_Bratton) and [Alonzo Highsmith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alonzo_Highsmith), Testaverde tossed four touchdown passes in a 28–16 win.[^cite_note-cane_mutiny-46][^cite_note-miami_oklahoma-80] Testaverde's performance led Oklahoma head coach [Barry Switzer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barry_Switzer) to remark that he had "never seen a better quarterback" in his 21 years with the Sooners, and at the conclusion of the regular season, Testaverde was awarded the [Heisman Trophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heisman_Trophy) with the fifth largest margin of victory in the voting's history.[^cite_note-testaverde_heisman-83]

Having seized the number one ranking with the win over Oklahoma, the Hurricanes finished the regular season at 11–0, outscoring their opponents 420–136, and accepted a bid to the [1987 Fiesta Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1987_Fiesta_Bowl) to play No. 2 [Penn State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1986_Penn_State_Nittany_Lions_football_team).[^cite_note-1987_fiesta-84][^cite_note-1987_fiesta2-85] There, the team's "outlaw" image grew when player [Dan Sileo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dan_Sileo) was doing an interviews in a Hells Angels jacket, plus arriving in Arizona clad in [fatigues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battledress). [Jerome Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerome_Brown) staged a walkout of a pre-game steak fry attended by both teams.[^cite_note-1987_fiesta-84] Before an estimated television audience of 70 million people, [Penn State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penn_State_Nittany_Lions_football) upset the heavily favored Hurricanes 14–10 to win the national championship, forcing seven turnovers, including Pete Giftopoulus' game-sealing interception of Testaverde in the end zone in the game's final seconds.[^cite_note-cane_mutiny-46][^cite_note-1987_fiesta-84][^cite_note-1987_fiesta2-85][^cite_note-1987_fiesta3-86]

#### 1987 season and second national championship

Led by [Michael Irvin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Irvin) and new quarterback [Steve Walsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Walsh_\(American_football\)), the [1987 Miami Hurricanes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1987_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team) won the school's second national championship and completed its first undefeated varsity season.[^cite_note-2007_media-10] The season was highlighted by one of the most memorable games in the history of the [Florida State–Miami football rivalry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_State%E2%80%93Miami_football_rivalry). Trailing No. 4 [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_State_Seminoles_football) 19–3 in the third quarter at [Doak Campbell Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doak_Campbell_Stadium), the Hurricanes rallied to take a 26–19 lead late in the fourth quarter on a 73-yard touchdown pass from Walsh to Irvin. Florida State responded with a touchdown in the final minute, but Seminoles head coach [Bobby Bowden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bobby_Bowden) opted to go for two points and the win rather than kick the extra-point for a tie, and Miami's Bubba McDowell broke up the conversion pass in the end zone to preserve the 26–25 victory.[^cite_note-87]

More than 60 players on the combined rosters for the game went on to play in the [NFL](21211.md).[^cite_note-1987_miami_fsu1-88] The 12–0 campaign was capped by a 20–14 win over the then-No. 1 [Oklahoma Sooners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oklahoma_Sooners_football) in an [Orange Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_Orange_Bowl) billed as "The Game of the Century".[^cite_note-miami_oklahoma-80] The win was Miami's third over Oklahoma in the last three seasons, accounting for Oklahoma's only losses during that time period.[^cite_note-miami_oklahoma-80]

#### 1988 season

The Hurricanes had a then-school record 12 players from the 1987 team selected in the [following spring's NFL draft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_NFL_draft),[^cite_note-cane_mutiny-46] including Irvin and [Bennie Blades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bennie_Blades), but with Walsh returning in [1988](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_NCAA_Division_I-A_football_season), the team gained the number one ranking with a season-opening 31–0 shutout of then-No. 1 Florida State at the Orange Bowl.[^cite_note-2007_media-10] The following week, Miami scored 17 points in the final 5 minutes and 23 seconds to top No. 4 [Michigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_Michigan_Wolverines_football_team) 31–30 at [Michigan Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michigan_Stadium).[^cite_note-89] Hopes of a repeat national championship were dashed, however, in the so-called [Catholics vs. Convicts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_Notre_Dame_vs._Miami_football_game) game, with Miami dropping an emotional 31–30 loss to [eventual-national champion Notre Dame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_Notre_Dame_Fighting_Irish_football_team) on a failed two-point conversion pass in the final minute.[^cite_note-cane_mutiny-46][^cite_note-1988_miami_nd-90][^cite_note-91]

Johnson left the program in February 1989 to become the head coach of the NFL's [Dallas Cowboys](8121.md),[^cite_note-92] ending his tenure at Miami with a 52–9 overall record and a 44–4 mark over his last four seasons.[^cite_note-93][^cite_note-cane_mutiny-46]

### Dennis Erickson era (1989–1994)

Despite having the support of students, players, and even the Miami police and fire departments, [offensive coordinator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Offensive_coordinator) [Gary Stevens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gary_Stevens_\(American_football\)) was bypassed for the head coaching job and athletic director [Sam Jankovich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_Jankovich) chose [Dennis Erickson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dennis_Erickson) of [Washington State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_State_Cougars_football) to succeed [Jimmy Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Johnson_\(American_football_coach\)) instead.[^cite_note-cm92-94]

#### 1989 season and third national championship

In [1989](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1989_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team), Erickson became just the second [Division I](669931.md) head coach to win a [national championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA_Division_I_FBS_National_Football_Championship) in his first season at a school.[^cite_note-2007_media-10][^cite_note-1990_sugar-95] Erickson's [1989 team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1989_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team), led by [Craig Erickson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Craig_Erickson) (no relation) at quarterback,[^cite_note-96] rebounded from a 24–10 mid-season loss at [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_State_Seminoles_football)[^cite_note-espn.com-97] and moved back into the national championship picture with a 27–10 win over then-top-ranked [Notre Dame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1989_Notre_Dame_Fighting_Irish_football_team) in the final regular-season game.[^cite_note-98] Miami's 33–25 win over No. 7 [Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1989_Alabama_Crimson_Tide_football_team) in the [Sugar Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugar_Bowl), combined with No. 1 [Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Buffaloes_football)'s loss to Notre Dame in the [Orange Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orange_Bowl), earned the program its third national championship.[^cite_note-1990_sugar-95][^cite_note-99]

On October 28, 1989, Miami mascot [Sebastian the Ibis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sebastian_the_Ibis) was tackled by a group of police officers for attempting to put out [Chief Osceola's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osceola_and_Renegade) flaming spear prior to Miami's game against long-standing rival [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_State_Seminoles_football) at [Doak Campbell Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doak_Campbell_Stadium) in [Tallahassee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tallahassee,_Florida). Sebastian was wearing a fireman's helmet and yellow raincoat and holding a fire extinguisher. When a police officer attempted to grab the fire extinguisher, the officer was sprayed in the chest. Sebastian was handcuffed by four officers but ultimately released. Miami quarterback [Gino Torretta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gino_Torretta), who started the game in place of injured [Craig Erickson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Craig_Erickson), told [ESPN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ESPN), "Even if we weren't bad boys, it added to the mystique that, 'Man, look, even their mascot's getting arrested.'"[^cite_note-ReferenceB-100]

Miami entered the [following season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1990_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team) as the number one team in the country, but a 28–21 upset loss to [Ty Detmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ty_Detmer) and No. 16 [BYU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1990_BYU_Cougars_football_team) in the opener derailed both the team's national championship chances and Craig Erickson's nascent Heisman campaign.[^cite_note-101] Later in the year, the Hurricanes lost to [Notre Dame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1990_Notre_Dame_Fighting_Irish_football_team) 29–20 in a game dubbed the "Final Conflict", as Notre Dame had decided to discontinue the 27-game rivalry,[^cite_note-nd_rivalry1-102] feeling the intensity of the series had reached an unhealthy level.[^cite_note-cane_mutiny-46] Miami ended the season with a 46–3 [Cotton Bowl Classic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Cotton_Bowl_Classic) victory over No. 3 [Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Longhorns_football) in the [1991 Cotton Bowl Classic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Cotton_Bowl_Classic) in which the team was penalized a bowl- and school-record 16 times for 202 yards, including nine unsportsmanlike conduct or personal foul penalties.[^cite_note-1991_cotton-103] On one play, [Randal Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Randal_Hill) scored on a 48-yard touchdown reception and continued to sprint out of the end zone and up the [Cotton Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cotton_Bowl_\(stadium\)) tunnel, where he then pretended to shoot at the Longhorns with imaginary pistols.[^cite_note-cane_mutiny-46] The program was widely criticized for its conduct, with [Will McDonough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Will_McDonough) of the *[Boston Globe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Globe)* likening the Cotton Bowl Classic display to a "wilding" and [Bill Walsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Walsh_\(American_football_coach\)) calling it "the most disgusting thing \[he'd] ever seen in college sports".[^cite_note-cane_mutiny-46] After the season, the [NCAA](60706.md) responded with the so-called "Miami Rule", which made it a 15-yard penalty to engage in excessive celebration or flagrant taunting.[^cite_note-cane_mutiny-46][^cite_note-104] Also during the off-season, Miami ended its 48-year status as an [independent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA_Division_I_FBS_independent_schools) and joined the [Big East Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_East_Conference_\(1979%E2%80%932013\)).[^cite_note-105]

#### 1991 season and fourth national championship

The [1991 Hurricanes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team) finished 12–0 and captured the program's fourth national championship in nine years behind quarterback [Gino Torretta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gino_Torretta) and a linebacking corps that featured [Jessie Armstead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jessie_Armstead) and [Micheal Barrow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Micheal_Barrow).[^cite_note-106] Miami's toughest test came in mid-November at then-No. 1 [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Florida_State_Seminoles_football_team) in the initial [Wide Right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wide_Right_I) game; with the No. 2 Hurricanes leading 17–16 in the final minute of the game, Florida State kicker Gerry Thomas' potential game-winning field goal attempt sailed "wide right" of the uprights.[^cite_note-nyt-107] Miami completed the second undefeated season in school history with a 22–0 shutout of No. 11 [Nebraska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Nebraska_Cornhuskers_football_team) in the [1992 Orange Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Orange_Bowl)[^cite_note-108] and finished first in the [AP Poll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AP_Poll), splitting the national championship with [Coaches' Poll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coaches'_Poll) champ [Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Washington_Huskies_football_team).[^cite_note-2007_media-10]

#### 1992 through 1994 seasons

[Hurricane Andrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Andrew) devastated much of [South Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Florida) in August 1992,[^cite_note-109] causing the program to relocate its preseason practice sessions north to [Dodgertown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dodgertown) in [Vero Beach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vero_Beach,_Florida).[^cite_note-2007_media-10] That season, [Miami went 11–0](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team) against the second-toughest schedule in the country,[^cite_note-110] topping No. 3 [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Florida_State_Seminoles_football_team) in [Wide Right II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wide_Right_II) and No. 7 [Penn State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Penn_State_Nittany_Lions_football_team) the following week in [Beaver Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beaver_Stadium).[^cite_note-2007_media-10] Meanwhile, Torretta became the second Hurricane to win the [Heisman Trophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heisman_Trophy), throwing for 19 touchdowns and 3,060 yards on the season and setting 11 school passing records during his career.[^cite_note-2007_media-10][^cite_note-111] Miami earned a trip to the [1993 Sugar Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1993_Sugar_Bowl), where the top-ranked and heavily favored Hurricanes were denied a repeat national championship by No. 2 [Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Alabama_Crimson_Tide_football_team), 34–13.[^cite_note-1993_sugar-112][^cite_note-1993_sugar2-113] The Sugar Bowl loss ended the program's 29-game winning streak, which dated to 1990.[^cite_note-1993_sugar2-113] The Hurricanes were frequently thrown off their rhythm by Alabama's 11-man fronts. Torretta threw three interceptions, one fewer than he had all season, in what would be the only loss of his collegiate career.[^cite_note-114] After the 1992 season, defensive coordinator [Sonny Lubick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sonny_Lubick) left to take the head coaching position at [Colorado State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_State_Rams_football).[^cite_note-115]

Although it was not apparent at the time, the Sugar Bowl loss marked the start of a downturn in Miami's fortunes. In [1993](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1993_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team), the Hurricanes lost three games in a season for the first time since 1984,[^cite_note-116] failed to win the Big East for the first time since joining in 1991, and was shut out in the [Fiesta Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_Fiesta_Bowl) by [Arizona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1993_Arizona_Wildcats_football_team), still the worst loss Miami has ever suffered in a bowl game.[^cite_note-2007_media-10] This led observers to wonder whether the Hurricanes were in decline.[^cite_note-cane_mutiny-46][^cite_note-warning-117]

In [1994](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team), Miami defeated [Georgia Southern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_Southern_Eagles_football) in the season opener for its 58th consecutive home win, setting an NCAA record.[^cite_note-118] The streak, which began in 1985, [was snapped](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whammy_in_Miami) two weeks later when [Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_Washington_Huskies_football_team) defeated the Hurricanes 38–20 at the [Orange Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami_Orange_Bowl).[^cite_note-streak_snapped-119] Led by [All-American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_Football_All-America_Team) defensive tackle [Warren Sapp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warren_Sapp)[^cite_note-120] and sophomore linebacker [Ray Lewis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ray_Lewis_\(American_football\)),[^cite_note-121] the team rebounded to earn a berth in the [1995 Orange Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1995_Orange_Bowl), where No. 1 [Nebraska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_Nebraska_Cornhuskers_football_team) outscored Miami 15–0 in the final quarter to win the game, 24–17, and the national championship.[^cite_note-2007_media-10][^cite_note-cm145-122]

With the threat of NCAA sanctions hovering over the program for a variety of infractions, Erickson stepped down after the 1994 season to become head coach of the NFL's [Seattle Seahawks](28390.md).[^cite_note-123][^cite_note-cane_mutiny-46] Erickson departed Miami with a 63–9 record over six seasons and the highest winning percentage (.875) and most national championships (2) of any coach in school history.[^cite_note-124][^cite_note-cm145-122]

### Butch Davis era (1995–2000)



Following a series of Miami Hurricanes football scandals, *[Sports Illustrated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sports_Illustrated)* published this cover story, "Why the University of Miami should drop football" on June 12, 1995



Hurricanes' running back [Edgerrin James](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edgerrin_James) (#5 in white jersey) against [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_State_Seminoles_football) at [Doak Campbell Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doak_Campbell_Stadium) in [1997](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team); in 2020, after an 11-year [NFL](21211.md) career, James was named to the [Pro Football Hall of Fame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro_Football_Hall_of_Fame).

Following Erickson's departure, Miami initially pursued former University of Miami defensive coordinator and then-[Colorado State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_State_Rams_football) head coach [Sonny Lubick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sonny_Lubick); however, he withdrew from consideration and opted to remain with the Rams.[^cite_note-125] Eventually, Miami settled on another former Hurricanes defensive assistant coach, then-[Dallas Cowboys](8121.md) defensive coordinator [Butch Davis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Butch_Davis).[^cite_note-126]

The Hurricanes finished [Davis's first season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1995_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team) with a record of 8–3.[^cite_note-127] However, on December 20, 1995, the NCAA announced that Miami would be subject to severe sanctions for numerous infractions within the athletic department.[^cite_note-nytimes.com-128] The Hurricanes were forced to sit out postseason play for the first time since 1982 and docked 31 scholarships from 1996 to 1998.[^cite_note-nytimes.com-128] Miami had actually self-reported the violations in 1991. However, when the Department of Education got word that school officials helped athletes fraudulently obtain [Pell Grants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pell_Grant), it asked Miami to stop its own investigation while it conducted its own. Ultimately, 60 athletes were implicated, but all of them avoided criminal charges after being sent through a pretrial diversion program.[^cite_note-Fanhouse-129]

In 1994, Tony Russell, a former University of Miami academic advisor, pleaded guilty to helping more than 80 student athletes, 57 of whom were football players, falsify [Pell Grant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pell_Grant) applications in exchange for kickbacks from the players themselves. The scandal dated all the way back to 1989 and fraudulently secured more than \$220,000 in federal grants. Federal officials later said that Russell had engineered "perhaps the largest centralized fraud ever committed" in the history of the Pell Grant program.[^cite_note-Wolff-130][^cite_note-131]

In late 1995, the NCAA concluded that, in addition to the fraudulent Pell Grants facilitated by Russell, the university had also provided or allowed over \$400,000 worth of other, improper payments to Miami football players. The NCAA also found that the university had failed to wholly implement its drug testing program, and permitted three football student-athletes to compete without being subject to the required disciplinary measures specified in the policy. The NCAA found that this was evidence that school officials didn't have adequate control over the football program.[^cite_note-132] Miami docked itself seven scholarships as part of a self-imposed sanction in 1995, and the [NCAA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA) took away another 24 scholarships over the next two years.

As a result of the scandal, *[Sports Illustrated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sports_Illustrated)*'s [Alexander Wolff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Wolff) wrote a famed and controversial cover story, arguing that Miami should at least temporarily shut down its football program.[^cite_note-Wolff-130]

On June 21, 1996, Miami football players broke into the apartment of the captain of Miami's track team and struck him repeatedly.[^cite_note-133] In response, Davis suspended three key players for the coming 1996 season,[^cite_note-134] in which the Hurricanes finished 9–3.[^cite_note-135] Davis also suspended two other players who were involved in separate violent incidents.[^cite_note-136]

The low point for Miami came in [1997](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team) when they posted a 5–6 record, the first losing season since Howard Schnellenberger's first year in 1979.[^cite_note-137] The 1997 season saw the Hurricanes suffer one of the program's most humiliating losses, a 47–0 beating at the hands of in-state rival [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_Florida_State_Seminoles_football_team).[^cite_note-1997_fsu1-138][^cite_note-1997_fsu2-139]

The Hurricanes began to reassert themselves in [1998](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team), when they finished 9–3.[^cite_note-140] In late September, Miami was forced to postpone their game with [UCLA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UCLA_Bruins_Football) due to [Hurricane Georges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Georges).[^cite_note-141] The game was rescheduled for December 5 and for the number 2-ranked Bruins, a trip to the national championship game was at stake. The Hurricanes rebounded from a 66–13 "caning" at the hands of [Syracuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syracuse_Orange_football) quarterback [Donovan McNabb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donovan_McNabb)[^cite_note-142] to put up over 600 yards of total offense against UCLA en route to a stunning 49–45 victory for the Hurricanes.[^cite_note-143]

[The following season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1999_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team) carried high hopes and expectations for the Hurricanes. They opened the year with a 23–12 win over [Ohio State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1999_Ohio_State_Buckeyes_football_team) in [East Rutherford](136878.md).[^cite_note-144] Early success, however, was tempered by tough losses to [Penn State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1999_Penn_State_Nittany_Lions_football_team)[^cite_note-145] and [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1999_Florida_State_Seminoles_football_team)[^cite_note-espn.com-97] during a three-game losing streak. The Hurricanes rebounded to win their last four games including a 28–13 win over [Georgia Tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_Tech_Yellow_Jackets_football) in the [Gator Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gator_Bowl).[^cite_note-146]

In [2000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team), Miami was shut out of the [BCS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bowl_Championship_Series) National Championship Game. Despite beating [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_Florida_State_Seminoles_football_team) head-to-head[^cite_note-147] and being ranked higher in both human polls, the Seminoles were chosen to challenge the [Oklahoma Sooners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_Oklahoma_Sooners_football_team) for the national championship.[^cite_note-148] The Seminoles were also chosen over [Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_Washington_Huskies_football_team), who also had one loss and who had handed Miami its only loss early in the season. Washington had been ranked third or fourth in the human polls, behind Miami. The Hurricanes went into the [2001 Nokia Sugar Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2001_Sugar_Bowl) as the Big East champions and, after much pregame antics including a brawl between members of the two teams on Bourbon Street in [New Orleans, Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans,_Louisiana),[^cite_note-149] defeated [Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_Florida_Gators_football_team) 37–20.[^cite_note-150]

On January 29, 2001, [Butch Davis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Butch_Davis) left Miami to become head coach of the [NFL](21211.md)'s [Cleveland Browns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleveland_Browns).[^cite_note-151]

### Larry Coker era (2001–2006)

After being turned down by [Wisconsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Badgers_football) head coach [Barry Alvarez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barry_Alvarez), athletics director [Paul Dee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Dee) promoted offensive coordinator [Larry Coker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Larry_Coker) to head coach to replace [Butch Davis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Butch_Davis).[^cite_note-152]

#### 2001 season and fifth national championship

Angered at being snubbed by the BCS, the Hurricanes stormed through the [2001 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2001_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team). They opened the season with a 33–7, nationally televised rout over [Penn State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2001_Penn_State_Nittany_Lions_football_team) in [Beaver Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beaver_Stadium).[^cite_note-153] Miami followed up the victory with equally decisive [Rutgers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rutgers_Scarlet_Knights_football),[^cite_note-154] [Pittsburgh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pittsburgh_Panthers_football),[^cite_note-155] and [Troy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Troy_Trojans_football).[^cite_note-156] After building up a 4–0 record, the Hurricanes defeated [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2001_Florida_State_Seminoles_football_team) in [Doak Campbell Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doak_Campbell_Stadium), 49–27, ending the Seminoles' 54-game home unbeaten streak.[^cite_note-157] The Hurricanes then defeated [West Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2001_West_Virginia_Mountaineers_football_team), 45–3,[^cite_note-158] and [Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_Owls_football), 38–0,[^cite_note-159] before heading to [Chestnut Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chestnut_Hill,_Massachusetts) to take on [Boston College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_College_Eagles_football). In the final minute of the fourth quarter, with Miami clinging to a 12–7 lead, Boston College quarterback [Brian St. Pierre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brian_St._Pierre) led the Eagles from their own 30-yard line all the way down to the Hurricanes' 9. With BC on the verge of a momentous upset, St. Pierre attempted a pass to receiver Ryan Read at the Miami 2-yard line. However, the ball deflected off the leg of Miami cornerback [Mike Rumph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Rumph), landing in the hands of defensive end Matt Walters. Walters ran ten yards with the ball before teammate [Ed Reed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ed_Reed) grabbed the ball out of his hands at around the Miami 20-yard line and raced the remaining 80-yards for a touchdown, resulting in an 18–7 Miami victory.[^cite_note-160] After surviving this scare, Miami shutout No. 14 [Syracuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syracuse_Orange_football), 59–0,[^cite_note-161] and defeated No. 12 [Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2001_Washington_Huskies_football_team), 65–7 in the Orange Bowl.[^cite_note-162] The combined 124–7 score set what the *[Orlando Sentinel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orlando_Sentinel)* described as an NCAA-record for the largest margin of victory over consecutive ranked opponents.[^cite_note-163][^cite_note-164]

The final hurdle to the [2002 Rose Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_Rose_Bowl) [BCS National Championship Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BCS_National_Championship_Game) was at [Virginia Tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Tech_Hokies_football). Miami jumped on Virginia Tech early, leading 20–3 at halftime, and 26–10 in the fourth quarter. But despite being outgained by the Hurricanes by 134 yards and being dominated in time-of-possession, the Hokies never quit. Virginia Tech added a couple of late touchdowns, attempting two-point conversions on each. The first conversion was successful, pulling them to 26–18, but receiver [Ernest Wilford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernest_Wilford) dropped a pass from quarterback Grant Noel in the end zone for the second conversion. Still, the resilient Hokies had one more chance to win the game late, taking possession of the ball at midfield and needing only a field goal to take the lead. But a diving, game-saving interception by [Ed Reed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ed_Reed) sealed the Miami victory, 26–24.[^cite_note-165] Defeating Virginia Tech earned the top-ranked Hurricanes an invitation to the [2002 Rose Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_Rose_Bowl) to take on BCS No. 2 [Nebraska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2001_Nebraska_Cornhuskers_football_team) for the national championship.

In the Rose Bowl, the Hurricanes took a 34–0 halftime lead and cruised to a 37–14 win over the Huskers to capture their fifth national championship and put the finishing touches on a perfect 12–0 season.[^cite_note-166] The Miami defense shut down [Heisman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heisman_Trophy) winner [Eric Crouch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eric_Crouch) and the vaunted Huskers offense, holding Nebraska 200 yards below its season average. [Ken Dorsey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ken_Dorsey) and [Andre Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andre_Johnson) were named [Rose Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rose_Bowl_\(game\)) co-Most Valuable Players.[^cite_note-167]

Six Hurricane players earned 2001 All-American status and six players were finalists for national awards, including [Maxwell Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maxwell_Award) winner, [Ken Dorsey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ken_Dorsey),[^cite_note-hurricanesports.com-168] and [Outland Trophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outland_Trophy) winner, [Bryant McKinnie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bryant_McKinnie).[^cite_note-169] Dorsey was also a [Heisman Trophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heisman_Trophy) finalist, finishing third.[^cite_note-hurricanesports.com-168]

The [2001 Miami Hurricanes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2001_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team) are considered by some experts and historians as one of the greatest teams in college football history.[^cite_note-170]

#### 2002 through 2006 seasons

Miami started the [2002 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team) as the defending national champion and the No. 1 ranked team in the country.[^cite_note-171] Behind a high-powered offense led by senior quarterback [Ken Dorsey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ken_Dorsey), new starting running back [Willis McGahee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Willis_McGahee),[^cite_note-172] and a stout defense anchored by [Jonathan Vilma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonathan_Vilma),[^cite_note-173] the Hurricanes completed their regular season schedule undefeated. The season was highlighted by a 41–16 win over rival [Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_Florida_Gators_football_team) at [Ben Hill Griffin Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Hill_Griffin_Stadium), the first regular season meeting between the rivals since 1987.[^cite_note-174]

The Hurricanes' toughest test was an October clash with in-state rival [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_State_Seminoles_football) at the [Orange Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami_Orange_Bowl). Miami overcame a 13-point second half deficit to defeat the Seminoles, 28–27.[^cite_note-175] The game was clinched when [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_Florida_State_Seminoles_football_team) kicker [Xavier Beitia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xavier_Beitia) missed a 43-yard field goal, wide left, as time expired.[^cite_note-176] Another signature win came four weeks later when Miami dominated the [Tennessee Volunteers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee_Volunteers_football), 26–3, before a crowd of 107,745 at [Neyland Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neyland_Stadium), considered one of the most hostile road venues in college football.[^cite_note-177] Miami would finish 12–0 and clinch a berth in the [Fiesta Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_Fiesta_Bowl) BCS National Championship Game after a wild 56–45 victory over Virginia Tech in which McGahee rushed for 205 yards and a school-record six touchdowns.[^cite_note-178] Both Dorsey and McGahee were named as finalists for the Heisman Trophy, finishing 4th and 5th, respectively.[^cite_note-179]

In the midst of a 34-game winning streak, Miami was a 13-point favorite going into the Fiesta Bowl match up against No. 2 [Ohio State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_Ohio_State_Buckeyes_football_team). The Hurricanes took an early 7–0 lead on a 25-yard touchdown pass from Dorsey to [Roscoe Parrish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roscoe_Parrish), but Ohio State seized control in the second quarter behind an aggressive pass rush, bolstered by constant [blitzing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blitz_\(American_football\)), and a stifling rush defense. The Buckeyes held a 14–7 lead at the half, and a field goal by [Mike Nugent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Nugent) extended Ohio State's advantage to 17–7 midway through the third quarter. A touchdown run by McGahee brought the Hurricanes within 3 points, but he suffered a knee injury early in the fourth quarter.[^cite_note-180] Miami was able to fight back and force overtime on a 40-yard field goal by Todd Sievers on the final play of the fourth quarter. Miami scored a touchdown on its first possession in overtime on a 7-yard pass from Dorsey to [Kellen Winslow II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kellen_Winslow_II), and, on Ohio State's ensuing possession, the Hurricanes appeared to have won the game, 24–17, after Buckeyes quarterback [Craig Krenzel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Craig_Krenzel)'s fourth-and-3 pass from the Miami 5 fell incomplete in the end zone. Miami players and coaches rushed the field and stadium fireworks were set off to commemorate the program's apparent sixth national championship. The celebration proved premature.[^cite_note-auto-181] At the conclusion of the play, Big 12 official Terry Porter threw a flag and made a controversial [pass interference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pass_interference) call against Miami cornerback [Glenn Sharpe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glenn_Sharpe).[^cite_note-auto-181] The penalty took the air out of Miami's sails and gave Ohio State new life, first-and-goal at the 1.[^cite_note-auto-181] The Buckeyes scored a touchdown to tie it at 24–24 at the end of the first overtime, and [Maurice Clarett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurice_Clarett)'s 5-yard touchdown run in the second overtime gave Ohio State a 31–24 lead.[^cite_note-auto-181] Miami's ensuing possession saw Dorsey briefly knocked out of the game after a hit from linebacker [Matt Wilhelm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matt_Wilhelm).[^cite_note-auto-181] After backup quarterback [Derrick Crudup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Derrick_Crudup) completed an 8-yard pass on third down, Dorsey re-entered and converted the crucial fourth-and-3 with a 7-yard completion to Winslow.[^cite_note-auto-181] Miami then drove to the Ohio State 2 yard-line, but was held to one yard on its next three plays, giving [Ohio State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ohio_State_Buckeyes_football) the national championship.[^cite_note-182]

Miami suffered through some offensive struggles in [2003](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team) behind new quarterback [Brock Berlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brock_Berlin). A blowout loss at [Virginia Tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Tech_Hokies_football) in early November ended Miami's 39-game regular season winning streak[^cite_note-183] and a loss the following week to [Tennessee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee_Volunteers_football)[^cite_note-184] ended Miami's national championship aspirations. The Hurricanes rebounded to win the Big East Conference championship and finish the season 11–2[^cite_note-185] with a [2004 Orange Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Orange_Bowl) victory over [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_Florida_State_Seminoles_football_team).[^cite_note-186]

Miami joined the [ACC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_Coast_Conference) in 2004.[^cite_note-187] Despite three conference losses, the Hurricanes ended the season with a [Peach Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Peach_Bowl_\(December\)) victory over rival [Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Florida_Gators_football_team).[^cite_note-188]

The [2005](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team) season marked the debut of [Kyle Wright](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyle_Wright_\(American_football\)) as Miami's starting quarterback, although the much-ballyhooed Wright would struggle with consistency during the season with much of Miami's success that year fueled by its defense.[^cite_note-189] After a loss to [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_State_Seminoles_football) after placekick holder Brian Monroe bobbled the snap for what would have been a game-tying field goal attempt,[^cite_note-190] Miami would win eight straight games, including a road win over No. 3 [Virginia Tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Tech_Hokies_football),[^cite_note-191] only to stumble two weeks later against underdog [Georgia Tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_Tech_Yellow_Jackets_football).[^cite_note-192] Miami's second conference loss of the season cost it a place in the inaugural ACC Championship game and it competed instead in the [Peach Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005_Chick-fil-A_Bowl), where it lost to [LSU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LSU_Tigers_football), 40–3.[^cite_note-193]

The Hurricanes went 7–6 during a [2006 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team)[^cite_note-194] that included an [on-field brawl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami-FIU_brawl) against [Florida International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIU_Golden_Panthers_football),[^cite_note-195] the shooting death of Miami defensive tackle [Bryan Pata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bryan_Pata),[^cite_note-196] and a four-game late-season losing streak. Only a [Thanksgiving](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thanksgiving_\(United_States\)) night victory over [Boston College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_College_Eagles_football), in Miami's last game of the regular season, saved the Hurricanes from a losing regular season record.[^cite_note-197]

The day following the Boston College victory, university president [Donna Shalala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donna_Shalala) fired Coker.[^cite_note-198] Coker coached through the postseason, where he won his final game, a 21–20 victory over [Nevada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nevada_Wolf_Pack_football) on December 31, 2006, in the [MPC Computers Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_MPC_Computers_Bowl).[^cite_note-199]

### Randy Shannon era (2007–2010)



[Randy Shannon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Randy_Shannon), head coach from 2007 to 2010



The Miami Hurricanes' defense (left in white jerseys) lining up against [Oklahoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oklahoma_Sooners_football) at [Oklahoma Memorial Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaylord_Family_Oklahoma_Memorial_Stadium) in September 2007

After a search that lasted two weeks, Miami athletics director [Paul Dee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Dee) named [defensive coordinator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defensive_coordinator) [Randy Shannon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Randy_Shannon), a starting linebacker on the 1987 national champions, as the Hurricanes' 22nd head coach on December 8, 2006.[^cite_note-200] Shannon reportedly agreed to a four-year deal worth over \$4 million.[^cite_note-201] His hiring made him the first [African American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_American) head coach in Miami football history.[^cite_note-202] One of Shannon's first acts as head coach was to impose a strict code of conduct for the team enacted in large part due to the misbehavior and arrests of Miami players during Larry Coker's tenure as head coach.[^cite_note-203]

Shannon's first year as University of Miami's head coach in [2007](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team) was one of the worst in the Hurricanes' modern history, with the team registering a losing 5–7 record.[^cite_note-204] Under Shannon, the team failed to reach a bowl game for the first time in a decade. Notably, it was the first time in 25 years that the Hurricanes had missed a bowl game with a full complement of scholarships.

Media draft experts considered the freshmen on the [2008 team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team) to be one of the top recruiting classes in the nation.[^cite_note-2008teamrankings-205] The 2008 season resulted in a 7–6 record.[^cite_note-206] The regular season was highlighted by losses to rivals [Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Florida_Gators_football_team),[^cite_note-207] and [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Florida_State_Seminoles_football_team),[^cite_note-208] and an upset victory over [Virginia Tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Virginia_Tech_Hokies_football_team).[^cite_note-209] The 26–3 loss to Florida was Miami's first in that series since 1985, snapping a 6-game winning streak against the Gators.
