Afterwards, the tension between the two teams was heightened when Shannon accused Florida coach [Urban Meyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_Meyer) of trying to run up the score with an unsuccessful deep pass into the end zone in the game's final minute.[^cite_note-210] The visiting Hurricanes were 221⁄2 point underdogs in the nationally televised game but only trailed 9–3 heading into the fourth quarter, leading some to wonder whether Meyer was trying to compensate for his team's unimpressive performance before kicking a [field goal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Field_goal_\(football\)) with \:25 remaining".[^cite_note-211][^cite_note-runupscore-212][^cite_note-213]

Miami was knocked out of [ACC Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_ACC_Championship_Game) contention with a late-season loss to [Georgia Tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Georgia_Tech_Yellow_Jackets_football_team) in which the Hurricanes surrendered the second-most rushing yards in school history (472).[^cite_note-georgiatech08-214] The Hurricanes finished the 2008 season with a 24–17 loss to [California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_California_Golden_Bears_football_team) in the [Emerald Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Emerald_Bowl).[^cite_note-215]

After the 2008 season, Shannon fired offensive coordinator [Patrick Nix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patrick_Nix), citing philosophical differences.[^cite_note-216] Also, starting quarterback [Robert Marve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Marve) left the team because he claimed not to be able to play for Coach Shannon.[^cite_note-217] Shannon placed strict restrictions on Marve's potential transfer destinations and received much criticism in the media.[^cite_note-218][^cite_note-219][^cite_note-220] However, the University of Miami claimed in a press release that the restrictions were set because of suspected tampering by Marve's family or others on behalf of the Marve family.[^cite_note-221]

Shannon's staff suffered more upheaval when defensive coordinator [Bill Young](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Young_\(American_football_coach\)) left to assume the same position at [Oklahoma State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oklahoma_State_Cowboys_football), his alma mater, in late January 2009. [North Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Carolina_Tar_Heels_football) assistant [John Lovett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Lovett_\(American_football_coach\)) was hired to replace him.[^cite_note-222] Shannon hired former [UMass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UMass_Minutemen_football) head coach [Mark Whipple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Whipple) as Miami's new offensive coordinator and associate head coach.[^cite_note-223] Several Miami offensive players from the 2008 season returned, including quarterback [Jacory Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacory_Harris), both starting running backs, most of the offensive line and its top six receivers.[^cite_note-224] Shannon has been able to recruit a number of Southern Florida's top high school football players by telling them that they would be able to play immediately. In fact, 21 [true freshmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/True_freshmen) played during the 2008 season opener.[^cite_note-225]

The [2009 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team) began on a poor note after two back up quarterbacks, Taylor Cook and Cannon Smith, both transferred out during fall practice, leaving the young Hurricane team with only one serviceable backup in true freshman [Alonzo Highsmith Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alonzo_Highsmith_Jr.)[^cite_note-226] Sophomore [Jacory Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacory_Harris) directed the newly implemented offense. To make matters worse, starting defensive end [Adewale Ojomo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adewale_Ojomo) suffered a broken jaw in a locker room fight that led to a season-ending injury, causing the already young Hurricane team to go into their season short handed.[^cite_note-227]

Miami faced a difficult schedule to start the 2009 season with visits to No. 18 [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_State), a home game against No. 15 [Georgia Tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_Tech), a visit to [Lane Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lane_Stadium) and the No. 7 Virginia Tech Hokies and a home visit from the defending [Big 12 Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_12_Conference) champions and BCS Champion runners-up in No. 3 [Oklahoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oklahoma_Sooners_football).[^cite_note-228] Some national media outlets and sites such as [ESPN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ESPN) predicted at best a 2–2 record for the Hurricanes with some even predicting an 0–4 start.[^cite_note-229] Miami opened up their 2009 season against [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_State_Seminoles_football) on [Labor Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labor_Day) night for a national broadcast for ESPN. Billed as a "Battle of Rebuilding Programs",[^cite_note-230] Quarterback Jacory Harris led a heroic comeback in Tallahassee to beat the then ranked Seminoles 38–34, overcoming a late interception and apparent injury to Harris in the 4th quarter.[^cite_note-231]

The next week, Miami welcomed the [triple option](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triple_option) offense of the No. 14 [Georgia Tech Yellow Jackets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_Tech_Yellow_Jackets) in yet another ESPN prime-time game. Georgia Tech came in hot off of a big ACC win against [Clemson University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clemson_University) the previous week and held a 4–0 record against the Hurricanes in the last four years, including the previous years pounding in Atlanta. The 2009 contest would be a different story all together, as the Hurricanes handily beat the Jackets 33–17 at home and allowed only 95 rushing yards in the process.[^cite_note-232] The next week, with the Hurricanes in the national spotlight for the first time in 5 years, the No. 9 Miami team visited the No. 11 [Virginia Tech Hokies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Tech_Hokies). In pouring rain, Tech defeated the Hurricanes by a final score of 31–7.[^cite_note-233] Beat up and embarrassed, Miami then played [Oklahoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oklahoma_Sooners_football). Without [Heisman Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heisman_Award) winner [Sam Bradford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_Bradford),[^cite_note-234] Oklahoma took an early 10–0 lead after two early Jacory Harris interceptions. Going into halftime, the Hurricanes trailed the Sooners 10–7 in a highly contested football game. Miami came out for the second with a huge hit on the kick off team by [Cory Nelms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cory_Nelms) that forced the Sooners to start inside their own 20. The following play, sophomore corner [Brandon Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brandon_Harris) hit Oklahoma Quarterback [Landry Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landry_Jones) and forced a fumble that eventually led to a Hurricane touchdown. The momentum stayed with the Hurricanes as they rode to a 21–20 win over the No. 8 team in the country.[^cite_note-235] Following the opening four weeks, Miami was 3–1 and was the talk of sports stations nationwide. Following the gauntlet first third of the season, the Hurricanes won against [Florida A\&M](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_A\&M) at home[^cite_note-236] and on the road against in-state foe [UCF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UCF_Knights_football),[^cite_note-237] moving all the way up to No. 10 in the polls. The Canes then had to take on [Clemson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clemson_Tigers_football) in [Miami](53846.md) in what was a contest of speed and athleticism. Turnovers, missed opportunities and stand-out back [C. J. Spiller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C._J._Spiller) led the Tigers to a 40–37 overtime win against the Hurricanes, knocking them out of BCS contention and putting the [ACC Championship Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ACC_Championship_Game) in serious jeopardy.[^cite_note-238]

A win against [Wake Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wake_Forest_Demon_Deacons_football) on [Halloween](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halloween) kept the Hurricanes in the conference race,[^cite_note-239] which they followed up on with a 52–17 defeat of [Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Cavaliers_football) in Miami.[^cite_note-240] The following week [North Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Carolina_Tar_Heels_football), led by former Miami head coach [Butch Davis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Butch_Davis), topped Miami 33–24 with an unimpressive performance by Jacory Harris and the offense.[^cite_note-241]

Miami finished the 2009 regular season with back-to-back wins over [Duke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duke_Blue_Devils_football)[^cite_note-242] and in-state opponent [South Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Florida_Bulls_football).[^cite_note-243] Miami's final record was 9–3, finishing in 3rd place for the [ACC Coastal Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ACC_Coastal_Division) behind [Georgia Tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_Tech_Yellow_Jackets_football) and [Virginia Tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Tech_Hokies_football).[^cite_note-244] The announcement of the 2009 bowl selections stirred some controversy. Instead of choosing the 3rd best team in the ACC (Miami), [Gator Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gator_Bowl) officials chose [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_State_Seminoles_football) to represent the ACC against [West Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Virginia_Mountaineers_football) instead of the Hurricanes because of the retirement of legendary FSU coach [Bobby Bowden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bobby_Bowden), who served as West Virginia's head coach from 1970 to 1975.[^cite_note-245]

The Hurricanes were relegated to the [2009 Champs Sports Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Champs_Sports_Bowl) in [Orlando, Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orlando,_Florida), to play against the 9–3 [Wisconsin Badgers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Badgers).[^cite_note-246] Though the Hurricanes were heavy favorites coming into the contest, the Badgers beat up on the Hurricanes consistently throughout the game. The Hurricanes started off fast with a big return to open the game by [Sam Shields](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_Shields), but the Canes just could not maintain any offense throughout the game and had no answer for the power offense of Wisconsin. Going into halftime, the Hurricanes trailed 17–7 and [Graig Cooper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graig_Cooper) blew out his knee on the poor turf just before halftime on a kick off return.[^cite_note-247] Though Miami scored a late touchdown and recovered the [onside kick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Onside_kick), they fell to Wisconsin 20–14 and finished the season at 9–4.[^cite_note-248][^cite_note-249]

After the 2009 season, Shannon signed the No. 13 recruiting class in the nation according to ESPN.[^cite_note-250] Shannon addressed many depth issues including offensive line, line backers and running backs, however the media claimed that the staff missed out on several of the more highly touted recruits on signing day, including a couple of "5 star" players.[^cite_note-251] Coaching changes were made before and after signing day, including the departure of defensive line coach/recruiting coordinator [Clint Hurtt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clint_Hurtt) to the [Louisville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisville_Cardinals_football)[^cite_note-252] and the loss of running backs coach [Tommie Robinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tommie_Robinson) to the [Arizona Cardinals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arizona_Cardinals).[^cite_note-253] Shannon replaced them with former Hurricane and [Kentucky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kentucky_Wildcats_football) defensive line coach Rick Petri[^cite_note-254] and running back coach Mike Cassano from [Florida International University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_International_University).[^cite_note-255] Subsequently, Shannon named wide-receiver coach [Aubrey Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aubrey_Hill) as the recruiting coordinator for the program.[^cite_note-256]

In May 2010, the university raised Shannon's pay and extended his contract as head coach through 2014.[^cite_note-257]

The Hurricanes finished the [2010 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team) with a 7–6 record,[^cite_note-258] which included losses to rivals [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_State_Seminoles_football)[^cite_note-259] and [Virginia Tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Tech_Hokies_football)[^cite_note-260] and their first ever loss to in-state opponent [South Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Florida_Bulls_football) in the last game of the season.[^cite_note-261]

Shannon was fired by athletics director [Kirby Hocutt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kirby_Hocutt) after the loss to South Florida.[^cite_note-262] Interim head coach [Jeff Stoutland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeff_Stoutland), who was offensive line coach under Shannon, led the team into its [2010 Sun Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Sun_Bowl) matchup versus [Notre Dame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Notre_Dame_Fighting_Irish_football); the Hurricanes lost the [New Year's Eve](277922.md) game 33–17.[^cite_note-263][^cite_note-264]

### Al Golden era (2011–2015)



[Al Golden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Golden), head coach from 2011 to 2015



Miami's defense (top in white jerseys) lines up against [Nebraska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nebraska_Cornhuskers_football) at [Memorial Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memorial_Stadium_\(Lincoln\)) in September 2014

On December 13, 2010, it was announced that athletics director [Kirby Hocutt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kirby_Hocutt) hired [Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_Owls_football) head coach [Al Golden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Golden) as the program's 23rd head coach.[^cite_note-265] Golden was regarded as an up-and-coming coach who had turned around an abysmal Temple football program.[^cite_note-266] Shortly after announcing Golden's hiring, Miami signed Golden to a five-year contract.[^cite_note-267]

In [2011](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team), Golden's first season, the Hurricanes posted a 6–6 record.[^cite_note-268] It was only the third time, since 1979, that the program had failed to register a winning record.[^cite_note-269] The Hurricanes kicked off the season with a 32–24 loss to [Maryland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maryland_Terrapins_football).[^cite_note-270] After upsetting No. 17 [Ohio State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ohio_State_Buckeyes_football),[^cite_note-271] the Hurricanes lost to [Kansas State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kansas_State_Wildcats_football) by a margin of 28–24.[^cite_note-272] Golden's team defeated in-state [FCS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Football_Championship_Subdivision) foe [Bethune-Cookman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bethune%E2%80%93Cookman_Wildcats_football) on October 1 by a score of 45–14.[^cite_note-273] After a close 38–35 loss to [Virginia Tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Tech_Hokies_football),[^cite_note-274] Miami defeated [North Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Carolina_Tar_Heels_football) by a score of 30–24[^cite_note-275] and No. 20 [Georgia Tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_Tech_Yellow_Jackets_football) by a score of 24–7.[^cite_note-276] The Hurricanes alternated between win and loss for the remainder of the season; losing to [Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Cavaliers_football) 28–21,[^cite_note-277] defeating [Duke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duke_Blue_Devils_football) 49–14,[^cite_note-278] losing to archrival [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_State_Seminoles_football) 23–19[^cite_note-279] beating [South Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Florida_Bulls_football) in a 6–3 defensive struggle[^cite_note-280] and losing to [Boston College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_College_Eagles_football) by a score of 24–17.[^cite_note-281]

On November 25, 2011, Miami signed Golden to a raise and four-year contract extension through the 2019 season.[^cite_note-282]

In [2012](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team), the Hurricanes finished with a 7–5 mark.[^cite_note-283] They started the season with a 41–32 victory over [Boston College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_College_Eagles_football) on September 1.[^cite_note-284] After a 52–13 blowout loss to [Kansas State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kansas_State_Wildcats_football),[^cite_note-285]

Golden's team won their next three; defeating [Bethune-Cookman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bethune-Cookman_Wildcats_football) 38–10,[^cite_note-286] [Georgia Tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_Tech_Yellow_Jackets_football) 42–36[^cite_note-287] and [NC State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NC_State_Wolfpack_football) 44–37.[^cite_note-288] Next, however, the Hurricanes lost their next three, dropping a 41–3 blowout to No. 9 [Notre Dame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Notre_Dame_Fighting_Irish_football),[^cite_note-289] an 18–14 struggle to [North Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Carolina_Tar_Heels_football),[^cite_note-290] and a 33–20 loss to No. 12 [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_State_Seminoles_football).[^cite_note-291] On November 1, Miami defeated [Virginia Tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Tech_Hokies_football) by a score of 30–12.[^cite_note-292] After a heartbreaking 41–40 loss to [Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Cavaliers_football),[^cite_note-293] the Hurricanes won their last two; dominating [South Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Florida_Bulls_football) 40–9[^cite_note-294] and outlasting [Duke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duke_Blue_Devils_football) by a score of 52–45.[^cite_note-295] This resulted in a three-way tie, with [North Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Carolina_Tar_Heels_football) and [Georgia Tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_Tech_Yellow_Jackets_football), for the best record in the ACC Coastal Division. [North Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_North_Carolina_Tar_Heels_football_team), which had defeated the Hurricanes earlier in the season, would have been declared the coastal division champion based on the ACC tie breaker formula.[^cite_note-miamiherald.com-296] However, due to [NCAA](60706.md) sanctions, they were ineligible for postseason play.[^cite_note-miamiherald.com-296]

Miami finished in second place based on the formula. However, due to likely pending NCAA sanctions from the [Nevin Shapiro scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_University_of_Miami_athletics_scandal), the university's administration preemptively chose to forego post-season play for the second consecutive year.[^cite_note-miamiherald.com-296] Had they played, it would have marked their only appearance in the ACC championship game, since joining the conference, in 2004.[^cite_note-miamiherald.com-296] It would also have set up a rematch with [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_State_Seminoles_football), who had defeated the Hurricanes earlier in the season.[^cite_note-miamiherald.com-296]

The Hurricanes compiled a 9–4 record in [2013](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team).[^cite_note-297] Golden's team came storming out of the gate, winning their first seven; a 34–6 win over in-state opponent [Florida Atlantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_Atlantic_Owls_football),[^cite_note-298] a 21–16 win over rival [Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_Gators_football),[^cite_note-299] a 77–7 thrashing of [FCS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Football_Championship_Subdivision) opponent [Savannah State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savannah_State_Tigers_football),[^cite_note-300] a 49–21 victory over [South Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Florida_Bulls_football),[^cite_note-301] a 45–30 win over [Georgia Tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_Tech_Yellow_Jackets_football),[^cite_note-302] a 27–23 close win over [North Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Carolina_Tar_Heels_football)[^cite_note-303] and a 24–21 nail biter over [Wake Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wake_Forest_Demon_Deacons_football).[^cite_note-304] The Hurricanes suffered their first loss of the 2013 season on November 2, losing to No. 3 [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_State_Seminoles_football) in a 41–14 thrashing.[^cite_note-305] Miami dropped a second straight game by way of a 42–24 loss to [Virginia Tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Tech_Hokies_football)[^cite_note-306] and a third consecutive loss to [Duke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duke_Blue_Devils_football) in a 48–30 disappointment[^cite_note-307] dropped the Hurricanes from a No. 7 national ranking to unranked in those three weeks. The Hurricanes were able to close out the regular season with two wins, defeating [Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Cavaliers_football) 45–26[^cite_note-308] and [Pittsburgh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pittsburgh_Panthers_football) 41–31.[^cite_note-309] Miami received a berth in the [2013 Russell Athletic Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Russell_Athletic_Bowl), a game they lost to No. 18 [Louisville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisville_Cardinals_football) in a 36–9 blowout.[^cite_note-310] In October 2013, after an investigation spanning two and a half years, the NCAA announced that "the committee acknowledged and accepted the extensive and significant self-imposed penalties by the university".[^cite_note-ncaa.org-311] Therefore, no further bowl ban would be enforced.[^cite_note-espn.com1-312] As a result, Miami was eligible to compete in ACC championship and BCS bowls for the 2013–14 season.[^cite_note-espn.com1-312] However, the NCAA stripped Miami of nine scholarships over three years.[^cite_note-espn.com1-312]

The Hurricanes went 6–7 in [2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team).[^cite_note-313] Miami kicked off the season with a 31–13 loss to No. 25 [Louisville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisville_Cardinals_football) on September 1.[^cite_note-314] Miami defeated [Florida A\&M](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_A\&M_Rattlers_football) 41–7[^cite_note-315] and [Arkansas State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arkansas_State_Red_Wolves_football) 41–20[^cite_note-316] over the next two weeks before losing to No. 24 [Nebraska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nebraska_Cornhuskers_football) by a score of 41–31.[^cite_note-317] On September 27, the Hurricanes defeated [Duke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duke_Blue_Devils_football) by a margin of 22–10.[^cite_note-318] On October 4, [Georgia Tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_Tech_Yellow_Jackets_football) defeated Miami by a score of 28–17.[^cite_note-319]

Miami won their next three, winning 55–34 over [Cincinnati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cincinnati_Bearcats_football),[^cite_note-320] 30–6 over [Virginia Tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Tech_Hokies_football)[^cite_note-321] and 47–20 over [North Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Carolina_Tar_Heels_football).[^cite_note-322] Golden's squad struggled to finish the season, losing their last four; a 30–26 letdown to archrival [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_State_Seminoles_football),[^cite_note-323] a 30–13 disappointment to [Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Cavaliers_football),[^cite_note-324] a 35–23 defeat at the hands of [Pittsburgh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pittsburgh_Panthers_football) in the regular season finale[^cite_note-325] and a 24–21 close defeat in the [2014 Independence Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Independence_Bowl) in [Shreveport, Louisiana](150473.md), to [South Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Carolina_Gamecocks_football).[^cite_note-326]

The Hurricanes finished 8–5 in [2015](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team).[^cite_note-327] By this time, many Miami fans had grown restless and irritated at the team's inconsistencies and began to call for Golden to be fired using different means, including flying airplanes over [Hard Rock Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hard_Rock_Stadium) with various "Fire Al Golden" banners.[^cite_note-328] The Hurricanes started the season with a 45–0 shutout of [Bethune-Cookman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bethune-Cookman_Wildcats_football) on September 5.[^cite_note-329] A 44–20 win over [Florida Atlantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_Atlantic_Owls_football)[^cite_note-330] and a 36–33 overtime victory over [Nebraska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nebraska_Cornhuskers_football)[^cite_note-331] followed before the Hurricanes lost 34–23 to [Cincinnati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cincinnati_Bearcats_football)[^cite_note-332] and 29–24 to No. 12 [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_State_Seminoles_football).[^cite_note-333]

Miami defeated [Virginia Tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Tech_Hokies_football) by a score of 30–20 on October 17 in what would be Al Golden's last win as Miami head coach.[^cite_note-334]

On October 25, 2015, the day after a 58–0 home loss to [Clemson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clemson_Tigers_football),[^cite_note-335] the worst defeat in school history,[^cite_note-336] the university's athletic director Blake James announced Golden's firing.[^cite_note-337] Golden was 32–25 in his five seasons at Miami and led the program to bowl games in 2013 and 2014.[^cite_note-338][^cite_note-339] [Tight ends](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tight_end) coach [Larry Scott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Larry_Scott_\(American_football\)) finished the season as interim head coach.[^cite_note-340]

In Scott's first game as interim head coach, the Hurricanes recorded a controversial win over [Duke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duke_Blue_Devils_football).[^cite_note-341] The Hurricanes used eight [laterals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lateral_pass) (reminiscent of the [1982 Cal-Stanford ending](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Play_\(American_football\))) on a kickoff return with no time remaining to score the game-winning touchdown and stun the Blue Devils by a score of 30–27.[^cite_note-sbnation.com-342] However, video evidence showed the play should have been blown dead and not counted as a touchdown, as Miami players who possessed the ball on that play's knee were shown to be on the ground more than once.[^cite_note-sbnation.com-342] Although the outcome of the game couldn't be changed, the [Atlantic Coast Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_Coast_Conference) subsequently suspended the game and replay officials for failing to catch the errors and make the correct call.[^cite_note-343]

On November 7, Miami defeated [Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Cavaliers_football) by a score of 27–21.[^cite_note-344] The next week, the Hurricanes lost to No. 17 [North Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Carolina_Tar_Heels_football) by a score of 59–21.[^cite_note-345] Miami then defeated [Georgia Tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_Tech_Yellow_Jackets_football) 38–21[^cite_note-346] and [Pittsburgh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pittsburgh_Panthers_football) 29–24.[^cite_note-347] The Hurricanes received a berth in the [2015 Sun Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Sun_Bowl), a game they lost to [Washington State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_State_Cougars_football) by a score of 20–14.[^cite_note-348]

### Mark Richt era (2016–2018)



[Mark Richt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Richt), head coach from 2016 to 2018

On December 4, 2015, former [Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_Bulldogs_football) head coach [Mark Richt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Richt) was named Miami's 24th head football coach.[^cite_note-349] The hiring generated much excitement and was well-received and praised all across the country.[^cite_note-350][^cite_note-351][^cite_note-352] Although he had recently been fired as head coach of the Bulldogs,[^cite_note-353] Richt achieved great successes during his 15 years as Georgia head coach. His teams represented the SEC in three BCS bowl appearances with a record of 2–1, and finished in the top ten of the final AP Poll seven times (2002–2005, 2007, 2012, 2014). His 2008 team also finished in the top ten of the coaches poll. His Georgia teams averaged about nine wins per season, won two [Southeastern Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeastern_Conference) championship games and reached four more, reached bowl games each of his 15 seasons as head coach and sent many players to [National Football League](21211.md) playing careers.[^cite_note-hurricanesports.com1-354]

Richt had prior ties to the Miami football program, having played [quarterback](25277.md) for the Hurricanes under [Lou Saban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lou_Saban) and [Howard Schnellenberger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard_Schnellenberger) from 1978 to 1982 and, despite being behind the likes of [Jim Kelly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Kelly), [Vinny Testaverde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vinny_Testaverde) and [Bernie Kosar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernie_Kosar) on the depth chart, amassed nearly 1,500 passing yards during his college playing career.[^cite_note-355] Richt also served as offensive coordinator at [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_State_Seminoles_football) from 1994 to 2000 under [Bobby Bowden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bobby_Bowden), overseeing an offense that was one of the most potent in the country, won two national championships, and produced two [Heisman Trophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heisman_Trophy) winners in [Charlie Ward](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlie_Ward)[^cite_note-356] and [Chris Weinke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Weinke).[^cite_note-357][^cite_note-hurricanesports.com1-354] Miami signed Richt to a five-year contract worth \$4.1 million annually.[^cite_note-358]

The Hurricanes improved to 9–4 in [2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team).[^cite_note-359] They began the season on September 3 by blowing out in-state [FCS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Football_Championship_Subdivision) opponent [Florida A\&M](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_A\&M_Rattlers_football) 70–3.[^cite_note-360] The next week, the Hurricanes defeated [Florida Atlantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_Atlantic_Owls_football) by a score of 38–10.[^cite_note-361] After defeating [Appalachian State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appalachian_State_Mountaineers_football) 45–10,[^cite_note-362] Miami defeated [Georgia Tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_Tech_Yellow_Jackets_football) by a score of 35–21 to record their first [Atlantic Coast Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_Coast_Conference) win under Richt.[^cite_note-363] Then, the Hurricanes embarked upon a four-game losing streak, dropping games to No. 23 [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_State_Seminoles_football) by a score of 20–19,[^cite_note-364] [North Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Carolina_Tar_Heels_football) by a margin of 20–13,[^cite_note-365] [Virginia Tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Tech_Hokies_football) by a count of 37–16[^cite_note-366] and [Notre Dame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Notre_Dame_Fighting_Irish_football) to the tune of 30–27.[^cite_note-367]

The Hurricanes rebounded to win their last five games of the season, a 51–28 trouncing of [Pittsburgh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pittsburgh_Panthers_football),[^cite_note-368] a 34–14 victory over [Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Cavaliers_football),[^cite_note-369] a 27–13 win over [NC State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NC_State_Wolfpack_football)[^cite_note-370] and a 40–21 win over [Duke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duke_Blue_Devils_football) with quarterback [Brad Kaaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brad_Kaaya) becoming Miami's all-time leading passer to close the regular season.[^cite_note-371] On December 28, 2016, Richt led the Hurricanes to their first bowl win in 10 years, when they defeated No. 16 [West Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Virginia_Mountaineers_football) in the [2016 Russell Athletic Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Russell_Athletic_Bowl) by a score of 31–14.[^cite_note-372]

Miami finished 10–3 in [2017](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team).[^cite_note-373] The Hurricanes began the season on September 2, defeating in-state [FCS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Football_Championship_Subdivision) opponent [Bethune–Cookman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bethune%E2%80%93Cookman_Wildcats_football) by a margin of 41–13.[^cite_note-374] The Hurricanes were supposed to play [Arkansas State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arkansas_State_Red_Wolves_football) on September 9, but the game was canceled due to [Hurricane Irma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Irma) battering the state of [Florida](18933066.md) that weekend.[^cite_note-375] Although the game was to be played in [Jonesboro, Arkansas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonesboro,_Arkansas), the University of Miami administration contended that it would be too difficult for the football team to safely travel in and out of Florida due to the intensity of the hurricane.[^cite_note-376] When Miami refused to reschedule the game and pay the \$650,000 they agreed to pay the Red Wolves, the Arkansas State University administration filed a lawsuit seeking the payment.[^cite_note-377] As a result of the cancellation, Miami only played 11 regular season games in 2017 as opposed to the usual 12.[^cite_note-378] Miami also rescheduled their game against [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_State_Seminoles_football) from September 16 to October 7 due to the aftermath of the hurricane.[^cite_note-379]

On September 23, Miami played its second game of the season, defeating [Toledo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toledo_Rockets_football) by a score of 52–30.[^cite_note-380] After a 31–6 victory over [Duke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duke_Blue_Devils_football),[^cite_note-381] Richt's team defeated archrival [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_State_Seminoles_football) by a score of 24–20.[^cite_note-382] After a 25–24 nail biting win over [Georgia Tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_Tech_Yellow_Jackets_football),[^cite_note-383] the Hurricanes defeated [Syracuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syracuse_Orange_football) by a margin of 27–19.[^cite_note-384] On October 28, Miami defeated [North Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Carolina_Tar_Heels_football) by a score of 24–19.[^cite_note-385] That was followed by a 28–10 victory over No. 13 [Virginia Tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Tech_Hokies_football).[^cite_note-386] On November 11, Richt's squad obliterated [Notre Dame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Notre_Dame_Fighting_Irish_football) by a score of 41–8.[^cite_note-387] After a 44–28 win over [Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Cavaliers_football),[^cite_note-388] Miami suffered its first loss of the season in the regular season finale, falling to [Pittsburgh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pittsburgh_Panthers_football) by a margin of 24–14.[^cite_note-389] In the [2017 ACC Championship Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_ACC_Championship_Game), Miami was obliterated by No. 1 [Clemson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clemson_Tigers_football) by a score of 38–3.[^cite_note-390] The Hurricanes accepted a berth in the [2017 Orange Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Orange_Bowl), a game they lost to No. 6 [Wisconsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Badgers_football) by a score of 34–24.[^cite_note-391]

On May 3, 2018, the University of Miami administration signed Richt to a five-year contract extension.[^cite_note-392] Miami ended 2018 with another loss to Wisconsin, this time in the Pinstripe Bowl 35–3, finishing 7–6. On December 30, 2018, Richt abruptly announced his retirement from coaching.[^cite_note-393]

### Manny Diaz era (2019–2021)



The Miami Hurricanes (left in orange jerseys) taking on [Louisiana Tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Tech_Bulldogs_football) in the [2019 Independence Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Independence_Bowl)

The University of Miami hired [Manny Diaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manny_Diaz_\(American_football\)) as their new head coach on December 30, 2018. A [Miami](53846.md) native, Diaz had previously been the team's defensive coordinator the previous three seasons.

Diaz had been hired as head coach by [Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_Owls_football) 17 days prior to Richt's retirement. On December 30, 2018, however, Diaz withdrew his commitment to Temple to accept the head coach opportunity at Miami.[^cite_note-Diaz-394]

Diaz compiled a 21–15 record as head coach during the 2019, 2020, and 2021 seasons. On December 6, 2021, Miami fired Diaz.

### Mario Cristobal era (2021–present)

On December 7, 2021, the University of Miami announced the hiring of [Mario Cristobal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mario_Cristobal), a former Miami Hurricanes lineman and member of two University of Miami championship teams in [1989](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1989_Miami_Hurricanes_football) and [1991](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Miami_Hurricanes_football), and the former head coach of the [University of Oregon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oregon_Ducks_football), as the new head coach.

In his first season, in [2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team), Cristobal had a 5–7 record and brought in a much improved recruiting class.[^cite_note-395] In Cristobal's second season as head coach, in [2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team), the team registered a 7–6 record and appeared in the [2023 Pinstripe Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Pinstripe_Bowl) at [Yankee Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yankee_Stadium), where it lost to [Rutgers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Rutgers_Scarlet_Knights_football_team) 31–24.

The 2024 team saw significant improvements over recent years and finished with a 10–3 record. Quarterback [Cam Ward](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cam_Ward_\(American_football\)) was a Heisman front runner all season long, and became the first overall pick in the [2025 NFL draft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_NFL_draft). However, the team experienced disappointment after missing out on both the [ACC Championship Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ACC_Championship_Game) and losing the [2024 Pop-Tarts Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Pop-Tarts_Bowl) to [Iowa State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Iowa_State_Cyclones_football_team) 42-41.

In [2025](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team), Miami took another step forward, with their best campaign in over 20 years. Miami got off to a hot start, defeating #6 [Notre Dame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Notre_Dame_Fighting_Irish_football_team). After beating in-state rivals [Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Florida_Gators_football_team) and [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Florida_State_Seminoles_football_team), Miami jumped up to second in the AP Poll. An at home upset loss to [Louisville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Louisville_Cardinals_football_team) and a road loss to [SMU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_SMU_Mustangs_football_team) sent Miami down to 18th in the polls.

Miami went onto win their last four games, and took the 10th seed in the [College Football Playoffs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_Football_Playoffs). This was their first College Football Playoff appearance. They started the playoffs with a 10–3 victory against [Texas A\&M](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Texas_A\&M_Aggies_football_team) on the road at [Kyle Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyle_Field), with freshman stud wide receiver [Malachi Toney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malachi_Toney) scoring a touchdown, then sealed by a [Bryce Fitzgerald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bryce_Fitzgerald) interception with A\&M deep in Miami territory. Miami moved onto the [Cotton Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Cotton_Bowl) where they faced off against the second seeded [Ohio State Buckeyes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Ohio_State_Buckeyes_football_team). Miami took a 10 point victory. They then went to the [2026 Fiesta Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Fiesta_Bowl) where they beat [Ole Miss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Ole_Miss_Rebels_football_team) on a last second touchdown scramble from [Carson Beck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carson_Beck).&#x20;

Miami made their first national title game since 2002, playing in the [2026 National Championship game](75819266.md) against the undefeated [Indiana Hoosiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Indiana_Hoosiers_football_team). Miami had a chance to score the go ahead touchdown with less than a minute left; however, an interception ended Miami's season.

In the 2026 transfer portal window, Miami added [Duke Blue Devils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duke_Blue_Devils) quarterback and wide receiver [Darian Mensah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darian_Mensah) and [Cooper Barkate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cooper_Barkate) and [Missouri Tigers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missouri_Tigers) edge rusher [Damon Wilson II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Damon_Wilson_II), among others. They are planned to be one of the top college football teams heading into the 2026 College Football Season.

## Conference affiliations

- [Independent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA_Division_I-A_independent_schools) (1927–1928)

- [Southern Intercollegiate Athletic Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Intercollegiate_Athletic_Association) (1929–1941)

## Championships

### National championships

Miami has been selected a winner of a [national championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_football_national_championships_in_NCAA_Division_I_FBS) nine times from NCAA-designated major selectors, for which the school officially claims five of them.[^cite_note-Walsh2007-396][^cite_note-2018ncaabook-397] Miami has won five national championships (1983, 1987, 1989, 1991, and 2001), which saw them finish number one in the final AP Poll each time.[^cite_note-398]

| **[2001](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2001_Miami_Hurricanes_football_team)** | [Larry Coker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Larry_Coker) | AP, [BCS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bowl_Championship_Series), FWAA, NFF, [USA Today/ESPN (Coaches)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coaches'_Trophy), | 12–0 | [Rose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_Rose_Bowl) ([BCS National Championship Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BCS_National_Championship_Game)) | **W** 37–14 | **No. 1** | **No. 1** |

## Bowl games

Main article: [List of Miami Hurricanes bowl games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Miami_Hurricanes_bowl_games)

Miami has played in 46 bowl games with a record of 21 wins and 24 losses in these 46 bowl games. Miami's most common bowl destination has been the [Orange Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orange_Bowl_\(game\)), where they have appeared nine times, compiling a 6–3 overall Orange Bowl record. Miami's most common opponent in bowl play has been [Nebraska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nebraska_Cornhuskers_football). The schools have met six times in bowl play with the Hurricanes winning four times and losing twice against the Cornhuskers.

## Rivalries

### Florida

Main article: [Florida–Miami football rivalry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida%E2%80%93Miami_football_rivalry)

See also: [Florida Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_Cup)

Miami's rivalry with [Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_Gators_football) dates back to 1938, making it the oldest rivalry among Florida's "Big Three": the University of Miami, the University of Florida, and [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FSU_football).[^cite_note-florida_rivalry2-400] The Hurricanes defeated the Gators, 19–7, in the first meeting between the geographic rivals.[^cite_note-florida_rivalry2-400] The Seminole War Canoe was carved in 1955 out of a cypress struck by lightning and was given to the winner of the annual football game. The canoe is meant to symbolize the fighting spirit of the Seminole people that is often on display during games between the Hurricanes and Gators. The canoe is now on permanent display at the University of Miami Sports Hall of Fame on the Coral Gables campus.

Miami holds the edge in the all-time series with a 29–26 record against Florida. The two schools met every year from 1944 until 1987, but have not played regularly since then. Florida canceled the annual series after the 1987 season,[^cite_note-florida_rivalry1-401][^cite_note-florida_rivalry2-400] when the requirement of the [Southeastern Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeastern_Conference) for member schools to play eight conference games induced the University of Florida to fill out the non-conference portion of its schedule with teams that do not require a home-and-home arrangement,[^cite_note-florida_rivalry2-400] except for Florida State.

From 1986 to 2003, Miami won all six of the games between the schools, including victories in the 2001 [Sugar Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugar_Bowl) and the 2004 [Peach Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peach_Bowl). Florida snapped its 23-year drought against Miami with a 26–3 win over the Hurricanes in 2008. In 2019, the series resumed with Florida winning 24–20 in the [Camping World Kickoff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camping_World_Kickoff) in Orlando, Florida.[^cite_note-402]

They most recently met on September 20th, 2025, at Hard Rock Stadium, Miami's home stadium in Miami Gardens, Florida. Miami delivered a convincing 26–7 victory over the Gators.

### Florida State

Main article: [Florida State–Miami football rivalry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_State%E2%80%93Miami_football_rivalry)

See also: [Florida Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_Cup), [Wide Right I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wide_Right_I), and [Wide Right II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wide_Right_II)



[Scott Covington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scott_Covington) passing against [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_State_Seminoles_football)'s defense at [Doak Campbell Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doak_Campbell_Stadium) in 1997

Miami's traditional rivals are [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_State_Seminoles_football)[^cite_note-1997_fsu1-138][^cite_note-1997_fsu2-139] and [Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_Gators_football).[^cite_note-florida_rivalry1-401][^cite_note-florida_rivalry2-400] Since 2002, the [Florida Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_Cup) has been awarded to the team that finishes with the best head-to-head record in years when Miami and Florida face each other.[^cite_note-403] To date, six Florida Cups have been awarded with Miami winning the first three.

Miami's rivalry with [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_State_Seminoles_football) dates to 1951 when the Hurricanes defeated the Seminoles 35–13 in their inaugural meeting. The schools have played every year since 1966, with Miami holding the all-time advantage, 33–30. Upon the conclusion of their 2003 regular-season schedules, the teams represented their respective conferences in the 2004 [FedEx Orange Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FedEx_Orange_Bowl) (Miami being the champions of the Big East, and Florida State being the champions of the ACC). Miami won the bowl game 16–14; it was the only time the schools have met in post-season football play. The 63 meetings between the teams of FSU and Miami eclipsed the rivalry between the Hurricanes and the Gators (from the [University of Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Florida)) following their 2010 game; the series of games between the University of Miami and Florida is Miami's second-longest at 55 games.

During the 1980s and 1990s, the series emerged as one of the premier rivalries in college football. Between 1983 and 2002, the Hurricanes and Seminoles combined to win 7 national championships and play in 14 bowl games with a national championship at stake. The 1988 game starred 57 future [NFL](21211.md) pros on the combined rosters. Since 2004, the year Miami left the [Big East Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_East_Conference_\(1979%E2%80%932013\)) to join the expanded 12-member [Atlantic Coast Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_Coast_Conference), the universities have been conference foes, though they are placed in separate divisions within the conference. This alignment creates the potential for the two teams to meet for a second time in the [ACC Championship Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ACC_Championship_Game), should each win their respective divisions in any particular season. Such a rematch has yet to happen after 14 years of ACC Championship Games, as of 2018.

The series has consistently drawn very high [television ratings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nielsen_ratings) with the 2006 Miami–Florida State game being the most-watched college football game—regular-season or postseason—in [ESPN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ESPN) history, and the 2009 and 1994 meetings being the second- and fifth-most watched regular season games, respectively.[^cite_note-ratings-404]

The Miami Hurricanes lead the all-time series 35–32 as of 2022. The most recent meeting was in 2022 on November 5, when the Seminoles won 45–3 in Miami, this is the largest margin of victory in the opposing team's stadium in the series history. Their next scheduled game is November 11, 2023.

### Louisville

Main article: [Louisville–Miami football rivalry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisville%E2%80%93Miami_football_rivalry)

The football rivalry between [Louisville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisville_Cardinals_football) and Miami dates back to 1933 and has been played 17 times.[^cite_note-405][^cite_note-406] The series became a conference rivalry when Louisville joined the ACC in 2014.[^cite_note-407] The programs are connected by head coach [Howard Schnellenberger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard_Schnellenberger), who resuscitated both schools' football programs and led both teams to great successes when they were struggling and facing financial issues.[^cite_note-408][^cite_note-409] A trophy was introduced to the rivalry in 2023 honoring Howard Schnellenberger, a bronzed pair of boots worn by Schnellenberger awarded to the winner of the game.[^cite_note-410][^cite_note-411][^cite_note-412][^cite_note-413] The Hurricanes lead the all-time series 12–5–1.[^cite_note-414][^cite_note-415][^cite_note-416]

### Nebraska

Main article: [Miami–Nebraska football rivalry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami%E2%80%93Nebraska_football_rivalry)

The series with [Nebraska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nebraska_Cornhuskers_football) is tied at 6–6 as of 2018.[^cite_note-417]

### Notre Dame

See also: [1988 Notre Dame vs. Miami football game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_Notre_Dame_vs._Miami_football_game) and [Catholics vs. Convicts (film)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholics_vs._Convicts_\(film\))



"Catholics versus Convicts" in the [2010 Sun Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Sun_Bowl)

The Hurricanes and the [Notre Dame Fighting Irish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Notre_Dame_Fighting_Irish_football) first met during the [1955 college football season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1955_college_football_season).[^cite_note-418] They met three times during the 1960s (1960, 1965 and 1967) and proceeded to play each other annually from 1971 to 1990 (except in 1986). Notre Dame consistently dominated the series in the 1970s. However, in the 1980s Miami began to dominate and the rivalry intensified significantly. Both teams were national contenders in the latter part of the decade, and both teams cost each other at least one national championship. Hostilities were further fueled when the Hurricanes routed the Fighting Irish in the 1985 season finale 58–7, with Miami widely accused of [running up the score](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Running_up_the_score) in the second half. The rivalry gained national attention again and both teams played their most famous games from 1988 to 1990. The game known as [Catholics vs. Convicts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_Notre_Dame_vs._Miami_football_game) was won by the Fighting Irish 31–30. In 1989, Miami ended Notre Dame's record 23-game winning streak with a 27–10 victory. The rivalry ended after the Fighting Irish dashed #2 Miami's hopes for a repeat national championship with a 29–20 victory at Notre Dame.

The two teams are scheduled meet in the following scheduled dates in the 2026, 2028, 2031, 2032, 2034, and 2037 seasons.[^cite_note-419]

### Virginia Tech

Main article: [Miami–Virginia Tech football rivalry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami%E2%80%93Virginia_Tech_football_rivalry)



Miami vs. [Virginia Tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Tech_Hokies_football) at [Lane Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lane_Stadium) in 2007

The Hurricanes and [Hokies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Tech_Hokies_football) first met on November 13, 1953, in Miami. The two teams have played annually since 1992, and the rivalry developed when the Hokies became a member of the Big East Conference for football in 1991. When the Atlantic Coast Conference (ACC) expanded in 2004, both Miami and Virginia Tech became members of the ACC. Both teams continued to compete annually as part of the ACC's Coastal Division. After the removal of divisions in the ACC, and a year break from the matchup in 2023, Miami and Virginia Tech have been scheduled as annual opponents through the year 2029. Miami and Virginia Tech have twice met in bowl games, the [1966 Liberty Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1966_Liberty_Bowl) and [1981 Peach Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1981_Peach_Bowl_\(January\)). As of 2024, Miami leads the series 25–15.[^cite_note-420] The last game between the two teams was September 28, 2024, in [Miami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami,_Florida), and Miami won 38–34 after a controversial decision by the referees following the final play.

The two teams will next play during a yet to specified date in the 2025 season at [Lane Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lane_Stadium).

## Facilities



[Hard Rock Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hard_Rock_Stadium) in [Miami Gardens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami_Gardens,_Florida), the home field for the Miami Hurricanes football team

[Hard Rock Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hard_Rock_Stadium) is the home field of the Hurricanes football team during their regular season. The stadium is located in [Miami Gardens, Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami_Gardens,_Florida), located approximately 22 mi (35 km) north of the University of Miami's main [Coral Gables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coral_Gables,_Florida) campus.[^cite_note-421] Hard Rock Stadium is also the home stadium of the [Miami Dolphins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami_Dolphins) of the [National Football League](21211.md).

At its inception, the Miami Hurricanes played at [Tamiami Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamiami_Park) and, later, at Moore Park. In 1937, they moved to the [Miami Orange Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami_Orange_Bowl) and played at the Miami Orange Bowl for seventy consecutive years, until 2007 when the stadium was designated for demolition.[^cite_note-422] The University of Miami then signed a 25-year contract for the team to play at Hard Rock Stadium through 2033.[^cite_note-BBC082107-423]

The Miami Hurricanes practice facilities are located on the [University of Miami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Miami) campus at Greentree Practice Fields, which was named the College Football Field of the Year by the SportsTurf Managers Association in 2007.[^cite_note-424] The Hecht Athletic Center, also located on-campus, serves as the program's training facility and is home to the football offices. In November 2010, the university announced a gift of \$5 million from the Ted and Todd Schwartz Family Foundation that was used to build the Schwartz Center for Athletic Excellence, which caters to over 400 student athletes and includes a sports medicine facility, training room, and academic center. The facility also has features specifically designed for the football team:

- The Paul J. DiMare Gallery of Champions, which showcases individual and team titles, such as the University of Miami's five national championships in football, as well as awards like the Heisman Trophies won by former Hurricanes Gino Torretta and Vinny Testaverde.

- A football locker room built following a gift from [Dwayne "The Rock" Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwayne_Johnson), who played with the Miami Hurricanes prior to his [WWE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WWE) and acting careers.

- An academic center with new computers, study rooms, and the 140-seat Robert & Lauren Mann Auditorium.

- An expansion of the existing Isadore Hecht Athletic Center to include a state-of-the-art sports medicine and training area.

  [^cite_note-425]

  [^cite_note-426]

In May 2017, the university broke ground on the new Carol Soffer Indoor Practice Facility. The \$34 million facility was the product of a \$14 million gift from Miami billionaire Jeffrey Soffer, owner of the [Fountainebleau Hotel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fontainebleau_Miami_Beach) in [Miami Beach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami_Beach,_Florida). The building exceeds 100,000 square feet and includes two turf fields that combine for 81,000-square feet. The indoor facility, located on the current football practice area, also houses a football operations center with a mezzanine level for football coaches' offices and meeting rooms.

## Records

Main article: [Miami Hurricanes football statistical leaders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami_Hurricanes_football_statistical_leaders)

### Winning streaks

Miami has two of the [longest winning streaks in NCAA Division I history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Longest_NCAA_Division_I_football_winning_streaks). From 2000 to 2003, Miami won 34 consecutive games, tying for sixth all-time, although it is the longest since 1957. The streak started on September 23, 2000, with a 47–10 victory at [West Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Virginia_Mountaineers_football) and ended on January 3, 2003, with a 31–24 double overtime loss to [Ohio State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_Ohio_State_Buckeyes_football_team) in the [2003 Fiesta Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_Fiesta_Bowl). Miami also won 29 straight games from October 27, 1990, to January 1, 1993, good for 13th on the all-time list. That streak was snapped when the top-ranked Hurricanes were upset by second-ranked [Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1993_Alabama_Crimson_Tide_football_team), 34–13 in the [1993 Sugar Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1993_Sugar_Bowl).

Miami owns the record for the longest home winning streak in NCAA history, winning 58 straight games at the Orange Bowl. The record streak began with a 38–0 shutout victory over [Cincinnati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cincinnati_Bearcats_football) on October 12, 1985, and ended with a 38–20 loss to [Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Huskies_football) on September 24, 1994. The 58 game streak includes three Orange Bowl victories where Miami was the away team because of the Orange Bowl's tie to the [Big Eight Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Eight_Conference).[^cite_note-427]

In addition to its own lengthy winning streaks, Miami has snapped four streaks of 20 games or more in its history. The only other school to snap four winning streaks of 20 or more games is [Princeton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princeton_Tigers_football).[^cite_note-428] In the [1984 Orange Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_Orange_Bowl), Miami ended top-ranked [Nebraska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nebraska_Cornhuskers_football)'s 22-game winning streak and won its first national championship with a 31–30 victory. The Hurricanes halted top-ranked [Oklahoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oklahoma_Sooners_football)'s 20-game streak and won their second national championship when they defeated the Sooners, 20–14, in the [1988 Orange Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_Orange_Bowl). The Hurricanes ended top-ranked [Notre Dame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1989_Notre_Dame_Fighting_Irish_football_team)'s 23-game winning streak with a 27–10 win on November 25, 1989. Miami also ended the 20-game winning streak of [UCLA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UCLA_Bruins_football) when Miami defeated the third-ranked Bruins 49–45 on December 5, 1998.

## Canes in the NFL

See also: [List of Miami Hurricanes in the NFL draft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Miami_Hurricanes_in_the_NFL_draft)



[Cam Ward](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cam_Ward_\(American_football\)), the first overall pick in the [2025 NFL draft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_NFL_draft)

Miami holds a number of [NFL draft](710502.md) records, including most first-round selections in a single draft and most consecutive drafts with at least one first-round selection.[^cite_note-draft_record-429] As of 2024, at least one University of Miami player has been selected in (49) consecutive NFL drafts, dating back to 1975,[^cite_note-ReferenceA-447] and (67) first-round picks the 5th most in college football. Miami has had (369) [players drafted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Miami_Hurricanes_in_the_NFL_draft), ranking 13th among all college football programs for the number of draft picks.[^cite_note-448]

Among all college football programs, the University of Miami holds the all-time record for the most [defensive linemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defensive_lineman) (49) and is tied with [USC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USC_Trojan_football) for the most [wide receivers](492476.md) (40) to go on to play in the NFL.[^cite_note-nfl.com-430]

## Traditions



[Sebastian the Ibis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sebastian_the_Ibis), the Miami Hurricanes' mascot

### Touchdown Tommy

Touchdown Tommy is the cannon that is fired off when the team runs out of the tunnel, after every point that the Hurricanes score, and at the conclusion of all team wins. The cannon is kept by the University of Miami's [Sigma Chi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigma_Chi) fraternity's Cannon Master and fired off during the games by senior brothers of Sigma Chi. Touchdown Tommy is the third-oldest tradition at the University of Miami after the [Iron Arrow Honor Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Arrow_Honor_Society), the highest honor awarded by the university founded in 1926, and [Sebastian the Ibis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sebastian_the_Ibis), the mascot of the [Miami Hurricanes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami_Hurricanes) since 1957.

### Smoke

One of the Hurricanes best-known traditions is the team's entrance scene. The team enters the field through a large cloud of white smoke billowing from its entrance tunnel, amid a tape of a hurricane blasting over the sound system. The smoke comes from fire extinguisher exhaust that billows from a series of pipes welded together by school transportation director Bob Nalette in the 1950s. In 2008, the pipes were moved from the [Miami Orange Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami_Orange_Bowl), the former home field for the Hurricanes, to [Hard Rock Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hard_Rock_Stadium), where the team has played since 2008.

Following the University Miami football program's rise to national prominence in the 1980s, many high school, college and [NFL](21211.md) teams over the last 25 years have copied this practice, and in 2001 [ABC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Broadcasting_Company) made a parody of it for a Saturday college football commercial.

### Ring of Honor



[Vinny Testaverde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vinny_Testaverde), 1986 [Heisman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Heisman_Trophy_winners) winner and part of inaugural "Ring of Honor" class

In 1997, the university established the 'Ring of Honor' as a way to honor outstanding players who have passed through the Hurricane football program.[^cite_note-450] Members are selected by an anonymous advisory committee, the director of athletics and the head football coach.[^cite_note-451] The names and jersey numbers of the inductees were displayed on the upper deck of the [Orange Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami_Orange_Bowl). The inaugural class of included [Jim Dooley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Dooley), [Ted Hendricks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ted_Hendricks), [George Mira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Mira), and [Vinny Testaverde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vinny_Testaverde). These four players are the only ones in the history of the program to have their numbers retired by the university as well.[^cite_note-452] In the 2015–16 season, Vinny Testaverde's retired number was unretired at his own request, allowing his son, [Vincent Testaverde Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vincent_Testaverde_Jr.), to wear No. 14 for the first time since its number retirement.

A second group of players consisting of [Ottis Anderson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottis_Anderson), [Don Bosseler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don_Bosseler), [Bernie Kosar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernie_Kosar), and [Burgess Owens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burgess_Owens) was inducted in 1999. After a nine-year hiatus, five new players were added in 2008: [Pro Football Hall of Famers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro_Football_Hall_of_Fame) [Jim Kelly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Kelly) and [Jim Otto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Otto), former [Heisman Trophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heisman_Trophy)-winner [Gino Torretta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gino_Torretta), running back [Edgerrin James](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edgerrin_James), and defensive lineman [Cortez Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cortez_Kennedy).

In 2009, [College Football Hall of Fame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_Football_Hall_of_Fame) defensive back [Bennie Blades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bennie_Blades), running back and former Miami head coach [Eddie Dunn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eddie_Dunn_\(American_football\)), and quarterback [Steve Walsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Walsh_\(American_football\)) were inducted.[^cite_note-453]

### Turnover Chain

In 2017, the Miami Hurricanes began to award its "Turnover Chain", an honor that contains a 36-inch, 2.5-kilogram, 10-karat gold chain, with 900 orange and green sapphire stones arranged in a "U" that is 6.5 inches wide.[^cite_note-454] It is presented during a game to any defensive player who forces a turnover.

Through the 11 regular-season games Miami played in 2017, the Hurricanes' defense created 29 turnovers (17 passes intercepted, 12 fumbles recovered), the third most in FBS football. During the same period, Miami gave up 12 turnovers (throwing 9 interceptions, and losing 3 fumbles); the net gain of 17 turnovers resulted in a margin of +1.55 per game. That figure represents the greatest margin in the nation this year, an achievement shared with [Central Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_UCF_Knights_football_team).[^cite_note-455]

In 2022, the Miami Hurricanes retired the chain.[^cite_note-456][^cite_note-457]

## Controversies and scandals

See also: [The U (film)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_U_\(film\))

The [University of Miami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Miami) football program has been at the center of several controversies, scandals and incidents, some of which have led to [NCAA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA) sanctions, suspensions, and negative publicity. In 1978, three University of Miami players threw a man into a lake, leading to the resignation of coach [Lou Saban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lou_Saban).[^cite_note-Feldman_2004_17–18-458] In the 1991 Cotton Bowl Classic, the team's unsportsmanlike conduct during the game gave rise to the so-called "Miami Rule". In 1995, the program was involved in a [Pell Grant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pell_Grant) scandal.[^cite_note-Wolff-130]

Other controversies and scandals involving the Miami Hurricanes football program include:

### 1980s: Luther Campbell's "pay for play"

In the 1980s, [2 Live Crew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2_Live_Crew) member [Luther Campbell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncle_Luke) was alleged to have been behind a "pay-for-play" system, which involved cash rewards to [University of Miami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Miami) players for scoring touchdowns, big hits, and other game accomplishments, though Campbell has never donated directly to the University of Miami or its athletic department.[^cite_note-459]

### 1989: University of Miami mascot detained by police

On October 28, 1989, University of Miami mascot [Sebastian the Ibis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sebastian_the_Ibis) was tackled by a group of police officers for attempting to put out [Chief Osceola's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osceola_and_Renegade) flaming spear prior to Miami's game against long-standing rival [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_State_Seminoles_football) at [Doak Campbell Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doak_Campbell_Stadium) in [Tallahassee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tallahassee,_Florida). Sebastian was wearing a fireman's helmet and yellow raincoat and holding a fire extinguisher. When a police officer attempted to grab the fire extinguisher, the officer was sprayed in the chest. Sebastian was handcuffed by four officers but ultimately released. Miami quarterback [Gino Torretta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gino_Torretta), who started the game in place of injured [Craig Erickson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Craig_Erickson), told [ESPN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ESPN), "Even if we weren't bad boys, it added to the mystique that, 'Man, look, even their mascot's getting arrested.'"[^cite_note-ReferenceB-100]

### 2004-05: 7th Floor Crew

In 2004, several Miami players recorded a 9-minute long rap song under the informal group name "7th Floor Crew". The song contained numerous lewd and obscene sexual references. It was later leaked to the public in 2005.[^cite_note-460] The song was set to the beat of [Aaliyah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aaliyah)'s "[If Your Girl Only Knew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/If_Your_Girl_Only_Knew)". [Bomani Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bomani_Jones) wrote about the incident after the recording was leaked.[^cite_note-461]

### 2006: FIU brawl

Main article: [FIU–Miami football brawl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIU%E2%80%93Miami_football_brawl)

On October 14, 2006, in the first meeting of cross-town programs, Miami and the [Florida International Golden Panthers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIU_Panthers_football) brawled after a [PAT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extra_point).

FIU cornerback Chris Smith wrestled Miami holder Matt Perelli to the ground after the kick and appeared to punch him in the chin. Another FIU cornerback, Marshall McDuffie Jr., kicked Perelli in the head.[^cite_note-462] Miami's [Anthony Reddick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_Reddick) swung his helmet at FIU players and Miami's [Brandon Meriweather](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brandon_Meriweather) kicked an FIU player. FIU's A'Mod Ned, who was injured, came onto the field and swung at Miami players with his crutches. The fight lasted just over one minute before the coaches of both teams were able to separate the players. Florida Highway Patrol state troopers and City of Miami police arrived on the field as the fight was going on and remained a visual presence on the sidelines and in the stands to prevent further fighting. The game was delayed approximately 15 to 20 minutes as the officials attempted to sort out which players were ejected and what, if any, penalty yards needed to be enforced.

The next day, 31 players from both schools were punished, including 18 from FIU and 13 from Miami. The Miami players were handed one-game suspensions while the FIU players were suspended for the remainder of the season. Three Miami players were suspended indefinitely and the rest were also assigned community service work.[^cite_note-463]

### 2002–2010: Shapiro scandal

Main article: [2011 University of Miami athletics scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_University_of_Miami_athletics_scandal)



[Nevin Shapiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nevin_Shapiro) (left) allegedly engaged in [rampant violations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_University_of_Miami_athletics_scandal) of [NCAA](60706.md) rules over eight years as a booster for University of Miami athletes.

[^cite_note-robinson-464]

[^cite_note-465]

A [Ponzi schemer](47185.md), convicted [felon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Felony) and former Miami booster, [Nevin Shapiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nevin_Shapiro), claimed he used investor funds to finance donations to the University of Miami's athletic program and gave an estimated \$2 million in illegal benefits to at least 72 current or former football and basketball players and coaches from 2002 to 2010. A [Yahoo! Sports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yahoo!_Sports) report alleged that Shapiro, through his donations, violated at least four major NCAA bylaws.[^cite_note-466]

During the investigation of the allegations, the university self-imposed a ban on bowl participation. In October 2013, the NCAA announced that the university would lose three football scholarships in each of the next three seasons, but there would be no further bowl ban. The university became eligible again for bowl participation as of the 2013–14 season.[^cite_note-ncaa.org-311]

## Documentaries

### *The U*

Main article: [The U (film)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_U_\(film\))

The Miami Hurricanes football program has been the subject of two widely viewed documentaries. In 2009, *[The U](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_U_\(film\))*, developed by [Miami](53846.md)-based studio [Rakontur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rakontur) was released and aired as part of [ESPN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ESPN)'s *30 for 30* film series.

According to the filmmaker, the University of Miami chose not to be involved in the project, and denied the film crew access to former coach [Randy Shannon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Randy_Shannon), former athletic director [Paul Dee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Dee), or former university president [Tad Foote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_T._Foote_II).[^cite_note-467]

The film features the rise of the University of Miami football program beginning in the 1980s and through the early 1990s during which the University of Miami won three national championships (1983, 1987, and 1991) followed by the program's subsequent fall from various national scandals related to the program and team, including player crimes and drug usage and privileges provided players through [Luther Campbell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luther_Campbell)'s alleged "pay for play" scheme in which the rap star reportedly paid University of Miami players for major hits and plays. The film addresses the controversial and pioneering nature of the University of Miami football program rise and fall during this era.

The film was pre-screened on the University of Miami campus on December 10, 2009, with only two student athletes attending.[^cite_note-468] Two days later, however, it aired globally on [ESPN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ESPN) following the [Heisman Trophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heisman_Trophy) award ceremony, drawing 2.3 million viewers and setting a record for the largest audience ever for an ESPN *30 to 30* episode.

### *The U Part 2*

Following on the success of *The U*, *The U Part 2* was developed and released, depicting a similar rise and fall of the University of Miami football program, this time from its 2002 national championship team, which is widely considered to be one of the best college football teams in the history of the sport, to yet another downfall featuring national scandals that cost the University of Miami football program scholarships and damaged its competitiveness for much of the early 2000s.
